2168. JOHN HALFPENNY was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of November, 50lbs. weight of lead, value 8 s.; the goods of David M'Intosh, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
MILES M'INNES . I am superintendent of Mr. David M'Intosh, of Bloomsbury-square. In November last he undertook to lay down gas-pipes in Holborn—the prisoner was in his service—on the 24th of November, about two o'clock in the afternoon, I was at the work—I noticed the iron pot and a pig of lead near it—the prisoner was at the pot—in about ten minutes I went to the place, and missed the lead from the pot, and the prisoner—from that time I did not see him till last Monday—it was worth about 2 d. a pound.
WILLIAM ATKINS . In November last I was in the service of Mr. M'Intosh. About two o'clock in the afternoon I missed a pig of lead, and the prisoner was gone.
Prisoner. Q. Had you seen me at work in the afternoon? A. Yes—when Mr. M'Innes sent me and some other men home for being drunk.
MILES M'INNES re-examined. I stopped the prisoner from work, because he was drunk—he remained on the work, but doing nothing—I told him not to work—on a sudden I missed both him and the lead.
WILLIAM KNIGHT . I was standing at the corner of French Horn-yard—I saw the prisoner take up a pig of lead, and go as far as Day and Martin's, and there put the lead on a wagon—he then took it to a cab, and told the cab —man to drive off.
JAMES BLAND . I am a cab-man. I drove off with the lead, at the prisoner's instance.
Prisoner. Q. Where did I leave it at? A. A marine-store shop.
GUILTY . Aged 41.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2169]
2169. PETER COOPER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 48lbs. weight of lead, value 12 s., the goods of David M'Intosh, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
ALFRED HUGHES ( police-constable D 174.) Between six and seven o'clock last Thursday evening, I stopped the prisoner in Devonport-mews, Paddington—he had a coat in front of him, and a basket behind him, and
the cord over his shoulder—I felt the weight of the basket, and said, "What have you got here; iron?"—he said, "Yes"—he then knocked me on the arm, and said he only took it to get a drop of beer—he said it was all right—I said I should like to see what was in the basket—he said, in fact he did not know what it was, or who put it there—I then searched the coat, which was in front of him—I found a ladle full of lead in one pocket, and in the other pocket three smaller pieces—they weighed 48lbs. —I said I should like to see what was in the basket—he said, "You can look over it, if you like, I shall be back in half-an-hour, we will have a drop of rum together"—I took him to the station—in going down Hampstead-road, I saw some ladles and melting lead, and in one of the ladles there are four marks, which correspond with marks on this lead—I have no doubt this lead came from that ladle.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where has this lead been locked up? A. At the station, in possession of the inspector, who gave it to me before I came out—I swear this is the piece of lead I took from the prisoner—I saw these marks on it as soon as I took it.
GEORGE BURLEY CUTTS . I am an engineer, working for Mr. David M'Intosh. The prisoner was working at the pipes in the Bayswater-road on Thursday—he was working there till about six o'clock—this is my ladle—it was on my work—the policeman fetched it away in the morning—I am not able to say whether this lead was melted in this ladle—it has been dipped out with this ladle, and left to cool.
Cross-examined. Q. Could you swear by this that it came out of that ladle? A. I could not swear to it—it has the appearance, from the impression on the lead.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you believe that lead had been scooped out by the ladle, out of the pot? A. I have no doubt of it—I would not have sworn it came out of the ladle unless I had seen it in the ladle—the marks on it seem to correspond very well.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2170]
2170. WILLIAM TAYLOR and WILLIAM JOYCE were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 20 yards of oil-cloth, value 3 l., the goods of Christopher Teesdale.
CHRISTOPHER TEESDALE . I am an oil-cloth manufacturer in Crawford-street. The prisoners were not in my employ—this cloth was taken from my premises—the officer showed it to me at the office—I know it to be mine—it was just inside the threshold of the front door.
PATRICK JENNINGS ( police-constable E 123.) I met the prisoners on the 15th of August, in Alsop-place, at a Quarter-past nine o'clock—Taylor had the oil-cloth on his shoulder, about a Quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's—he carried it about thirty yards, then Joyce took it, and carried it on—I asked where they were taking it—they said, "To a cigar-shop in Regent-street"—they did not know the number—I left Joyce with the oil-cloth, and took Taylor to the house—when I got near the place he ran off—I took him, and went back, and got Joyce and the cloth.
Taylor. We were tipsy at the time—we never went into Crawford-street—we picked it up at the corner of Gloucester-place—it was lying against the railing.
(Joyce received a good character.)
TAYLOR— GUILTY . Aged 21.
JOYCE— GUILTY . Aged 23.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2171]
2171. JOHN WIGLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of July, 1 ring, value 15 s.; and 1 snuff-box, value 5 s.; the goods of Thomas Wells.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Mary Wells.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
MARY WELLS . I am separated from my husband. Early in July, the prisoner came to my shop, which is in Chancery-lane—I had been washing my hands—I placed my ring over my knuckle—he caught it Quickly off my finger—I requested him to return it—he would not—he went very quickly out of the shop—I saw it when it was produced by the pawnbroker—I repeatedly asked the prisoner for it after he took it—he said he had left it with a friend.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you been in the shop? A. Not more than three weeks—I have been separated from my husband fifteen years—he is alive—my last child is between thirteen and fourteen—I do not know the prisoner extraordinarily well—I never drank with him—he once brought a coffee-biggen over to me, and told me how I should make coffee—I did not have any—he has been in the habit of coming in and sitting down, though he was not asked—it was on law business that he should have been employed, but I found he was no lawyer—I never trusted him with any other articles—I allowed him to take some cigar-cases to sell for me—he did not return them—I never gave him the ring—I never said he borrowed an opal-ring before that—there was a blue ring he asked me to lend him, laying he was going to a gentleman's party, and wished to make a dash with it—I let him take it on proviso that he let me have it the same evening—he brought it the next morning—he took this one so suddenly I could not prevent it—I written to his sister—the letter produced is my hand-writing—he borrowed the other a few days before this—if I sent this letter it was after he was taken into custody—he did not borrow this ring—I have not said he borrowed it—I have said, if he did not return it it would be the worse for him—I should not have taken these proceedings if he had returned it—the ring was given me by the father of my child.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you give him leave to pawn your property? A. No.
ARTHUR JAMES JONES . I am assistant to a pawnbroker. I produce this ring, which was pledged for 7 s. by a person very much like the prisoner —I am not sure it was he that pledged it—I gave him this duplicate.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the date of the pledge 1 A. The 29th of July.
JAMES DAVIES ( police-constable T 112.) I took the prisoner on the 3rd of August—I did not search him—he gave me two duplicates—one is for this ring.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410823-2172]

JOHN TOWNSEND . I am a butcher in Hungerford-market. The prisoner was in my service about six months—this mutton is mine.

MARY ANN BEESTON . I live with Mr. Burrell, in Burton-crescent., I take bread of the prosecutor—the prisoner used to deliver it—on the 24th of January I paid him 1 s. 9 d.; on the 27th of February, 1 s. 9 d.; and on the 19th of June, 1 s. 9 d.—here are the receipts he gave me.

2179. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 1 hat, value 20 s., the goods of Abraham Harris.
ABRAHAM HARRIS . I am a clothier. I lost my hat on Sunday night week, the 15th of August, at Mr. Garrett's coffee-room, which is opposite my house—it was a pretty good hat—I laid it on the table, and when I looked for it to gap away, it was gone—the prisoner was stopped with it on his head—he had left a very shabby one instead of it, which is two sizes too small for me.
Prisoner. I was not stopped with it on my head. Witness. Yes, I took it off your head.
JAMES FAULKNER . I was in Mr. Garrett's parlour, and I saw the prisoner come in—he went round the table, and put his hat alongside of Mr. Harris's, and when he was going out he brought Mr. Harris's hat on his head—he had got about half way across the room—he was not out.
Prisoner. I was a mistake—I discovered it, and went back, and gave it to Mr. Harris.
GUILTY .

2180. JOHN JONES was again indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of May, 1 hat, value 15 s.; 1 pair of gloves, value 4 s. 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief, value 3 s.; the goods of William Jones.
WILLIAM JONES . On the 23rd of May I was at the Castle public-house, in Whitechapel—I hung up my hat, which had a pair of gloves and a handkerchief in it—when I came to look for them, they were gone, and an old hat left in the place of mine—I lost my hat altogether—I did not observe the prisoner there.
JOHN TAPSCOTT . I keep the Castle public-house. On the 23rd of May I was sitting drinking a glass of grog with a friend—the prisoner came in, and called for a glass of ale—I served him—he then went out backwards, and returned—before he went he hung up his hat—when he came back he took the handkerchief out of the prosecutor's hat, and wiped bis face with it—he then doubled up the handkerchief, put it on his forehead, put the hat on, and went out—I looked up soon after, and saw an old hat in the room of it—I am certain he is the person—the prosecutor went out, but could not find him.
Prisoner. I never was in the house in my life.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.

2182. JOSEPH WARD was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of August, 1 wheelbarrow, value 10 s., the goods of William Rubergall, his master.
WILLIAM RUBERGALL . I live by the water-works at Pimlico. The prisoner was in my employ as carter—this wheelbarrow was taken out of
my yard—I went last week, and identified it at Mr. Davls's yard—when I gave the prisoner in charge he said he did not do it.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How long had he left your service? A. Five or six weeks after I discharged him I found the barrow—I had no Quarrel with him—he used to collect dung for me—this barrow is worth 3 s. now—I lost it twelve months ago—it was then worth 10 s.
THOMAS DAVIKS . I am a cow-keeper. This barrow was found on my premises—the prisoner used to wheel my dung out—he said he had an old barrow at home, which was broken, and if I would put it in repair, be would sell it me—I said, "Let me see it"—I gave him half-a-crown for it, and repaired it.
Cross-examined. Q. You did a great deal to it? A. Yes—it was done in my yard—another man, who worked for me, fetched the barrow.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined One Month.

2190. ELLEN MORIARTY was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of July, 2 sovereigns, the monies of John Butler, from his person.
JOHN BUTLER (a blind man). I live in Maynard-street. About six o'clock in the evening of Saturday, the 31st of July, I was going to my room up stairs—I found a hand in my trowsers' pocket—I could not tell whose hand it was at the time, but I laid hold of it, and put my hand down, and found three sovereigns in the person's hand—I pushed one down—she took up my hand, and put ray finger into her mouth, and bit it in four places—I kept her, and held her till the witness and the policeman came—I had four sovereigns in my pocket.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not try to get in doors when half the door was broken in—there were six persons took you up stairs? A. No, I did not—I was not drunk.
MARY RILEY . I heard a cry of "Robbery"—I ran, and picked up two sovereigns.
JEREMIAH TANNING . I heard Butler cry out "Robbery"—I gave him assistance—the prisoner was hustling with him, and trying to bite his finger—I saw Riley pick up the sovereigns where the prisoner was—I saw the prisoner bite the prosecutor's finger, and she tried to bite me several times.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not come in with Butler and Joe! A. No.
JOHN HUNTLEY ( police-constable P 143.) I was called in, and took the
prisoner—I did not see any fighting—the prosecutor laid on the ground—I took the prisoner from Tanning—Riley gave me two sovereigns—the prosecutor said, "Ellen, you have got my money"—she said, "Yes, I took it, and am d—d sorry I did not keep it."
Prisoner. When I went into the watch-house Butler said, "I am robbed"—I said, "Search him," and they found two sovereigns and a half, and 8 s. in another pocket.
JOHN BUTLER . No, she had three sovereigns in her hand—when I took hold of her hand in my pocket I pushed one out of her hand, and she dropped two.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2191]

2192. JOHN RUSH was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of August, 1 scarf, value 10 s.; and 1 shawl, value 5 s.; the goods of James Richmond Folkard.
EDWIN HAM . I am shopman to James Richmond Folkard, a pawnbroker, in Upton-place, Commercial-road. At one o'clock in the morning on the 25th of August I was sitting up in bed watching the door—I saw the prisoner putting his hand through the fan-light, and dragging this shawl and scarf through the window—I went out of a side door, went round, and saw the prisoner and another man—I presented a pistol at them—they both ran—I ran after them—the other crossed the road—I followed the prisoner, and Murphy stopped him—I was behind him—I found this shawl and scarf at the foot of the door under the prisoner's feet—they are my master's.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not say that the other got away with the shawl? A. No.
EDWARD MURPHY . Between one and two o'clock in the morning I was coming up Sutton-street—I met the prisoner running as fast as he could—I caught him—there was a cry of "Stop thief—when I took him he said he had not robbed any of them—I said it looked very likely by his running away.
FINLEY WENTWORTH . I was in bed in the shop—I ran out after Ham—I picked up the shawl about three feet from the door—I saw the prisoner running.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going home; this man came and stopped me; there was another man running over the way; I had been robbing no man: the shopman came up, and put a pistol to my head, and said I had robbed him; I asked of what; he said he did not know; they took me to the, shop, and showed me a small place over the window, about two inches wide and four or five long.
JURY to EDWIN HAM. Q. How did he get to the fan-light? A. On the knob of the door there was a mark of mud—I slept in the shop—he had got his hand and arm in.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Nine Months.

2194. JOHN CARR, JAMES MERCER , and JAMES CLARK , were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, 1 cap, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of William Saunders.
WILLIAM SAUNDERS . I keep a shop in High-street, Shadwell. On the evening of the 16th of August, between eight and nine o'clock, I missed a cap from my door—it had been tied to a string—the policeman came—I went to the station-house and found the cap—this now produced is it.
JOHN RANSOM ( police-constable K 328.) On the 16th of August I saw Carr coming along with two others—Carr had something tied in a handkerchief—I asked what he had got—he said a cap, which was his own, and he had bought it of Mercer, who was with him—Mercer said, "What a story, I am sure you did not"—I took them to the station—Carr then said, "Now I will tell the truth about howl came by this cap; James Clark and Mercer brought it down Twine-court and offered it to me and my uncle for sale for 2 s., and I went to get money on it to pay for it"—I went and took Clark—at the Thames-police Mercer said he did it for hunger—Clark said he had got a bad leg, and his father said if it did not get better he must go and do as Mercer did for his living.
Carr's Defence. I was at work all day in the docks; in the evening Mercer and Clark came and offered the cap to me and my uncle for sale; I was going to get money on it.
NOT GUILTY .

2195. JANE FREEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, 1 pair of spectacles, value 14 s.; and 1 spectacle-case, value 1 s., the goods of William Midgley, from his person.
WILLIAM MIDGLEY . I am a house-agent, and live in Rawstorne-street, On the 14th of August, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I was returning home through St. Lukes, and was pinioned by three or four women—I tried to make my way out, but in doing so I lost my spectacles out of my waistcoat-pocket—these are them—I was not drunk—I had had a pint of half-and-half and two glasses of rum—I was sober enough to walk home.
Prisoner. He said at the Magistrate's that he had beeji accosted by five or six women in Old-street, and treated them with gin. Witness. No, the spectacles were taken from my pocket; I did not give drink to any women.
GEORGE HEALEY . About half-past twelve o'clock that night I was in Chiswell-street—the prosecutor passed me and appeared very much intoxicated, which drew my attention—I returned back and saw the prisoner attack the prosecutor—I saw her hand in his pocket—I took hold of her—my friend had to assist the policeman to take her to the watch-house, and in going along my friend took the spectacles out of her hand.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked them up at the corner of Type-street—two or three of them asked me what I picked up, and said, "Give it to me," but I would not, as if I had, they would have kept it.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Confined Three Months.

JAMES MILREA . I saw the prisoner that evening—he asked me how I was—he had some kind of garment under his arm, but I could not see what.

GEORGE KING ( police-constable H 111.) PHILIP SMITH . I saw the prisoner take twelve 2lb. loaves, and one 4lb. loaf out of the prosecutor's basket, put them into a basket, and take it with him—I followed him till he put it down—he took one out, and I took him.

[trialAccount t18410823-2208]
2208. THOMAS PEARCE was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Henry Deal, on the 4th of August, and stabbing and wounding him in and upon the left side of his body, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
HENRY DEAL . I am a shoemaker, and live at Limehouse. On Wednesday afternoon, the 4th of August, between three and four o'clock, I went to the Robin Hood and Little John beer-shop, the prisoner was there when I went in—I remained there till between ten and eleven o'clock at night—between seven and eight we were in the parlour, the prisoner, my father-in-law, one Dixon, and Jewell and myself—the prisoner had some words with Thomas Dixon, and said he would punch his b—head in pieces —I replied, "No you won't, because he is not able to stand before you;" as Dixon was a cripple—I said if he bit him, I would hit him again, as he was not able to stand against him—he said he did not care for any man in Poplar who was 9 stone 3lb. weight—after this a wrangle took place between him and me, but it was dropped for some little time, and we afterwards went from the parlour into the tap-room—we still kept wrangling for some time afterwards, and all in an instant he jumped up, and swore d—his eyes if he would not have revenge on me—he got up, and made an attempt to hit me with his hand—I did not notice whether it was shut or open, but I kept it off with my left hand—I then struck him with my right hand, and hit him on the nose, which bled—I afterwards hit at him with my right hand, and I received a pain in my side—the prisoner struck at my side before I felt the pain—at the time I thought it was with his fist—I felt a little pain in my side—after that my father-in-law got up and said, "Don't strike him any more"—I said I would not—we were still quarrelling—I afterwards went out backwards to ease myself, and felt myself very sick—I then returned into the tap-room—he began still rather to wrangle—I drank out of the pot with my father-in-law—he and I went out the front way, and we had a few words out in the street—I went directly to my father-in-law's house, and when he came home I told him I felt rather queer in my side—I remained in an arm-chair, resting, until the morning (I did not live with him) between four and five o'clock I felt myself a great deal worse—I had no waistcoat on, and I looked down, and saw the blood on my shirt—I asked my father-in-law to look, and there was a wound in my side—I looked at it myself, and saw a hole in my side, and a hole in my shirt, and in the waistband of my trowsers—my father-in-law dressed,
and went for a policeman—the parish doctor came down and looked at me—they took me to the hospital directly—I remained there until now—no body else had struck me a blow in the course of the day—when the prisoner struck me I felt the pain in the same part as the wound is—I had not got the wound when I went into the Robin Hood and Little John—I had not seen any thing in the prisoner's hand—I had seen him once or twice before.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How many of you were there in the public-house? A. Five of us—my father-in-law was there—he other persons were not my friends—I had been drinking with Dixon before—Jewell was the fourth person, but I believe he was out the greatest part of the time—I found the prisoner in the house when I went in—he appeared then to have been drinking a great deal, he was not very sober then—he drank beer several times with my father-in-law—Dixon did not use gross language to him in my presence—at first, I had no idea I was wounded, and did not inform the prisoner of it—a bread-and-cheese knife has been found, which is supposed to have inflicted the wound—I do not know if he used it for bread and cheese—( produced)—this is the knife that was produced afterwards—I did not notice any eating going on during the time—there might have been, but I did not see any.
JURY. Q. What time did you leave the public-house? A. I suppose it was between ten and eleven o'clock—I did not discover the wound until after four o'clock the next morning—I was not very sober at night—I was in the morning when I looked at it.
ROBERT AYLIFFE . I am a City policeman. I saw the last witness on the morning of the 5th of August, and saw his wound—his shirt was all over blood with the cutting, and also the waistband of his drawers—I afterwards went to Robin Hood-lane, and there found the prisoner in a house—I took him into custody, and told him he was charged on suspicion of stabbing a man—I did not say what man—he said it was a bad job, he could not help it—I took him to the station, and took the prosecutor to the hospital.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he say, if he had stabbed a man, he must have been drunk, and knew nothing of it? A. Not to me.
THOMAS HOLMES . I am a police-sergeant. The prisoner was brought to the Poplar station—I sent Ayliffe for the parish doctor—while he was gone, I asked the prisoner if he had got a knife—he said, "Yes," and gave it to me—I opened it, and perceived blood on the blade—I said, "It is my opinion, Pearce, that you are guilty of the offence you are charged with; you need not say any thing unless you like; if you do, it may be used as evidence against you elsewhere"—he said, "If I did stab the man, I don't remember any thing of it"—I said, "Was you drunk then?"—he said, "I was a great deal the worse for liquor, for I had been drinking some time"—the charge was then taken—I went to the house, and saw the prosecutor in bed—I fitted the knife to the hole in the waistband of his trowsers and his shirt, which answered exactly to it—it was exactly the same size.
WILLIAM BOUSFIELD PAGE . I am a surgeon at the London Hospital. I remember the prosecutor being brought to me on the morning of the 5th of August—I examined his left side, and found a wound of about half an inch long, or rather less—it was just below the ribs—it was a part likely to inflict a serious injury, if it had been as deep as the symptoms led me to suppose it was—the symptoms were those of inflammation—he is still under my care—he has not yet perfectly recovered—I saw this knife at
the police-office—I have not compared it with the wound—I imagine the wound was done with an instrument of this sort.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not take any means to ascertain the depth of the wound? A. No—I apprehend a wound remaining a considerable time not attended to would be rendered more difficult to cure, the inflammation would increase, and make the wound very much worse, the symptoms would be aggravated—a blow inflicted with full force would have made a much worse wound—the prosecutor was intoxicated, and had lost some blood—I believe he fainted—it might possibly have been cured earlier if it had not been neglected—a wound inflicted when a person is intoxicated might have a more serious effect than otherwise.
HENRY DEAL re-examined. Q. You say you struck the prisoner on the nose? A. Yes—I was about hitting him again, when I felt the pain—the wound is on the left side—I struck at him with my left hand—I stood in front of him to strike—I did not notice what he was doing with his hand at the time.
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18410823-2209]
2209. WILLIAM MILLER was indicted for feloniously assaulting John Samuel Fisher, on the 21st of August, with intent his goods and monies, from his person, and against his will, violently and feloniously to steal.
JOHN SAMUEL FISHER . I shall be fifteen years old next October—I am clerk to Mr. Robert Hudson, Custom-house agent, St. Dunstan's-hill—I have been seven or eight months in his employment—he has an office in the lobby of the Custom-house. Last Saturday week I had occasion to pay 16 l. 3 s. 6 d. into the treasury of the Custom-house—it was a 10 l. note, a 5 l. note, a sovereign, a half-crown, and a shilling—it was Mr. Hudson's money—I had to go across the long room up a small passage to the treasury—I had the money in my hand—I crossed the long room with it, and got about two steps from the door of the treasury—there are a great many people in the long room—I did not see any body near the treasury door—while I was about to enter the door a person came behind me, and gave me several severe blows on the head, and afterwards knocked me down and demanded my money—he said, "Give me your money" three or four times when I was down—I would not part with it—I held it firmly in my hand, and called out "Murder"—while I was calling out I heard somebody approaching, and then the person who attacked me ran away—he had a blue coat on—I did not notice his features—I noticed his coat when he was about a dozen yards from me, after he turned to run away—I am certain of his having a blue coat—two or three moments after he ran away Cook and Kelly came up—I told them which way the man had gone—I was carried into the treasury, and sent for my employer, and then I put the money on the table—two or three minutes after Cook and Kelly came up, while I was sitting on the chair in the treasury, I heard somebody call out, "He is caught, he is caught"—I came out of the treasury, and saw the prisoner in custody—he had a blue coat on—my father came to the place afterwards, and said to him, "You vagabond, or scoundrel, how came you to hit a boy like that?"—he said, "I have not hurt him, I have not hurt the boy"—I have suffered a good deal from the violence, and I have been attended by a doctor.
THOMAS KELLY . I am a messenger in the employ of the Customs. On Saturday week, about half-past twelve o'clock, I was in the bench lobby near the treasurer's office—I heard a great screaming in the passage, and a cry of "Murder," repeated two or three times—I went to see what was the matter, and about two paces from the boy I saw a man in a blue coat running—I immediately ran after him—I lost sight of him, at he turned round a corner—there are several corners there—I only lost sight of him once, and I never regained sight of him till I found him in a coal-hole, not far from the staircase—there were some gentlemen standing on the top of the staircase—he must have been twenty paces from the staircase when I lost sight of him—he would have two corners to turn to get to that staircase—there was no way for him to get out of the Custom-house but by that staircase—in consequence of something that passed with those gentlemen, I came back, and looked in the coal-cellar—I had ran past the coal-cellar to get to the top of the stairs—the coal-cellar is about eight or ten paces from the top of the stairs—after passing the coal-cellar I should have to pass one of the corners I have mentioned—a person going into the coal-cellar would be concealed from a person on the top of the stairs by the corner—a man named Salter ran as far as the prosecutor, who was lying down when we passed him—no other person went on with me—I then went to the coal-cellar, and found the door was not as it generally is when I have occasion to go there—I pulled the door open, went in, put my foot in behind the door, and felt a man—I then fastened the door back so as to keep him behind it, and hallooed out to Mr. Salter, who was coming up, that I had got him—the person I seized was the prisoner—he had on a blue coat—I had not heard at that time any thing from the boy about the person who assaulted him—I secured him, and said, "I have got you"—he said, "I know you have"—I then handed him over to Mr. Brunet, the constable—he was taken into a little lobby near the coal-cellar, and there kept some time—the boy was brought to him and shown to him—I did not hear him say any thing then.
JAMES COOK . I am clerk in the Receiver-general's office in the Custom-house—I was in the office about noon on Saturday week last, about half-past twelve o'clock—I heard a noise in the passage, and a boy calling out "Oh!" several times—I took no notice of that, as it is sometimes customary with boys—after that I heard a call of murder—I went out immediately into the passage, and as I was going out I saw a man pass the door—I did not see enough of him to take notice of his dress—I went into the passage and found the boy there—he was partially rising, and apparently in great agony—I assisted him up, and asked him to walk into the office—he said he could not—I carried him in, and placed him on a chair—he requested his employer might be sent for, which was done, and about one or two minutes afterwards the prisoner was taken into custody—the boy's father was sent for, and when he came he said to the prisoner, "You scoundrel, or rascal, how dare you ill use the boy in the way you have done?"—he said "I have not ill-used the boy"—the police were sent for, and on their arrival he said there was no need of so much fuss, for he would go without any resistance.
JOHN SALTER . I am a messenger to the Bench officers of the Customs—last Saturday week I was standing in the Bench lobby, and heard a violent scream of "Murder" in the passage—I immediately went into the passage, and saw the boy just rising from the ground, with his hat in his hand, very
much crushed, and saw a man running from him—I merely saw his back, he had on a blue coat with gilt buttons—on going round the first corner I lost sight of him and Kelly also—I afterwards heard Kelly call out, "Salter, I've got him"—I went to the coal-hole and said, "Do not open the door till I have got a candle"—when I returned with one several persons had come up, and the prisoner was out of the coal-hole—I heard him say that he went into the coal-hole to make water—I examined the corner where he had been standing, but there was no moisture whatever—there was nothing about his dress which had the appearance of his having opened his dress to make water.
COURT. Q. Did you see any other person when you saw the man with the blue coat running away? A. There was no one in the passage at the time—there is no other outlet from the passage except that by which I was coming and the man running—there is no other door in the passage in the way he ran but the coal-hole, into which he could have turned.
WILLIAM HENRY MARSHALL . I am clerk to Messrs. Henderson, of Mincing-lane. I was at the Custom-house in the Treasury about half-past twelve o'clock on Saturday week—I heard a noise in the passage—I at first thought it was some boys at play—I then heard screams and a cry of "Murder"—I opened the Treasury door and went out—as I did so I saw somebody rush by the door—when I got into the passage I found Fisher in a stooping position, as if he was getting up, and his hat was on the ground, very much crushed—several persons came up, and the boy was taken into the Treasury—shortly after I heard a cry that they had found a man—I afterwards saw the prisoner in the passage—the boy saw him, and said in his hearing he believed he was the man.
JOHN FISHER . I am the father of the boy. I was at work at Galleyquay, and was sent for to the Custom-house last Saturday week, and saw the prisoner there in custody—I said to the prisoner, who was then in custody, "You scoundrel," or something similar, how dare you knock the boy about in this kind of manner, seeing the boy in a terrible way—he said, "I have not hurt him"—he was then taken away in custody.
ROBERT HAMLIN ( City police-constable, No. 539.) I was sent for to the Custom-house last Saturday week, a little before one o'clock—I found the prisoner there in custody—some one asked him why he secreted himself in the coal-cellar—he said he had been there to make water—I examined the coal-cellar, but there was not the least appearance of any such thing having taken place—I afterwards remarked in his presence at the station that there was no such appearance—he said nothing.
WILLIAM BROWN ( City police-constable, No. 543.) I took the prisoner from the Custom-house to the station—I afterwards took him from the Mansion-house to the Computer—as we were going along I remarked that he appeared to have several witnesses against him—he replied that some of the witnesses were right enough, but one or two he considered of no use at all.
THOMAS KELLY re-examined. I could not see the stairs at the time I lost sight of the man I was pursuing—I could not see the stairs from the cellar-door—it is on the first floor—the two gentlemen were standing at the top of the first flight, on the same floor as the cellar—at the bottom of the stairs there is a passage leading into the street—I knew the persons who were standing at the top of the stairs—neither of them were dressed in a blue coat.
Q. By the time you got to the top of the stairs, would there have been time for a person to hare run down those stairs and got away by the passage, without your seeing him? A. No—I did not see any one running down those stairs, or along the passage below—the person could not have run down those stairs—I do not think he could have run round the second corner before I ran round the first.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to the Custom-house, on Saturday, with a young man I was acquainted with, who had some business to transact there—he left me waiting while he went into one of the offices—while standing there (and I believe the last witness stated at the Mansion-house that he saw me standing there ten minutes) I saw two or three persons go to this coal-cellar, which I thought was for the sake of convenience, and I went in there; while I was there the person came and said, "Look, he has gone in there;" I have been in a most respectable situation up to this last fortnight, and I have not made my friends acquainted with this.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2210]
2210. MICHAEL MURPHY was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Thomas Connor.
JEREMIAH HAGERTY . I am a ballast-getter. I knew the deceased, Thomas Connor, he was also a ballast-getter—on the 5th of June he was at the Ballast-office, in Narrow-street, Ratcliff—he was very nearly drunk, and not in a fit state to go to work—I was in the street—we went down a passage leading from the street to the water-side, and when half-way down the passage he was told he was too late—we returned back—I and a person named Power were joking together, and in passing by, I tapped Connor at the back of his neck with a cinder—he turned round and asked what I did that for—I begged his pardon, and said I did not do it designedly—he said I ought to mind where I threw things—he stood for a minute, then turned round to the prisoner and uttered some words; I could not understand what they were, but the last word he said was "Peter"—the prisoner was standing opposite to me, with his two hands in his pockets—as soon as the words were spoken, he up with his fists and struck Connor under the left ear—he fell to the ground, with his head on the stones close to my feet—his head turned to the right side, and blood flowed from his ear—he was carried to Mr. Buchanan's, the doctor's, at the corner of Ratcliff-cross, Ratcliff-highway—I saw no more of him till I saw him at the inquest, at the London Hospital—I then saw his dead body—I do not know whether he and the prisoner were acquainted before—Connor did not attempt to strike the prisoner before he struck this blow—I could not tell whether the language Connor used to the prisoner was abusive or not, for I had ray head turned the wrong way—Connor's head came against the ground first when he fell—he fell on the back part of his head—"Peter" is a nickname of the prisoner's—he is offended at it—he has often got upbraided with that name at public-houses.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had the prisoner been drinking? A. I do not think he had—I could not swear it—he might have had a little, but not much—this was about five o'clock in the afternoon—I had not drank any thing that day, nor for three weeks before—it was a cold cinder I hit Connor with—I had it in my hand before he came into the passage—I was skylarking with Power about a pipe of tobacco—I just pitched the cinder out of my hand—it made him angry—I was looking
at Power when I heard the word "Peter," and when I turned round again, I saw the prisoner strike him—Burgess and Smith, ballast-heavers, were there also—Power was not so near as me—I swear the prisoner was not the worse for liquor—he might have had two or three pints of beer, but that would not make him tipsy—Connor was not a friend of mine particularly—I was a mate of his for two years, in the same craft—the prisoner had worked with us five or six months—I never had any quarrel with him.
PATRICK SMITH . I am a ballast-heaver. On the 5th of June, I was standing at Ratcliff-cross, and saw Connor going down the ballast-office passage—I saw a cinder strike him in the head as he was going down—I did not see who threw it—the prisoner was standing outside with his back to the wall—he said, "Connor, come out"—some other words passed between them which I did not hear—I saw Connor turn his head round towards the prisoner—the prisoner stepped out from the wall, and hit him with his right-hand on the left ear—I did not see Connor strike at the prisoner before the prisoner struck him—Connor fell down, and when he was lying down, the prisoner walked towards where he was lying, changed colour and shed tears, and said he was very sorry for it—I saw no other blow than I have mentioned—Connor appeared in liquor—I did not suppose he was angry when he went up to the prisoner—when he fell his head went on one of the paving stones of the street—he was carried to the doctor's shop.
JOHN POWER . I saw part of the transaction—I did not see the blow struck—I was standing talking to Hagerty—I saw no blow struck on either side—I saw Connor fall on his back, and saw blood coming out of his ear.
GEORGE ADAMS . I am a surgeon. I attend at the London hospital—the deceased was brought there on Saturday, the 5th of June—he was in a state of insensibility—I examined his head, and found a quantity of blood flowing from his ear—I found no mark of any blow outside—he was in the hospital till the 13th of June, and then died—he continued insensible the whole time—I afterwards examined his body—his skull was fractured, and the brain torn considerably—there was a quantity of blood in the base of the brain—before I opened the head, I examined to see if there were any external marks—I attribute the immediate cause of his death to pressure on the brain from the blood—the appearances I saw might undoubtedly have been occasioned by a blow or fall—it was the lower part of the skull that was fractured, behind the ear—it would very likely be occasioned by the head coming in contact with a stone—I should think it almost impossible it could have been caused by a blow from a man's fist—it was no doubt a recent injury—I do not remember seeing the witness Hagerty at the hospital—I saw him before the Coroner when Connor's body was there—it was the same body I had examined.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 36.— Strongly recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Years.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.

2211. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of August, 29 yards of woollen cloth, value 5 l., the goods of Soren Samuel Palm.
SOREN SAMUEL PALM . I live in High-street, Shadwell. On Saturday night, the 21st of August, about a quarter before ten o'clock, this cloth was at my door—I saw it move, ran immediately to the door, and saw the prisoner with it on his shoulder—I followed him across the road, laid hold of him, and brought him back into the shop with it—it is worth 5 l.
Prisoner. As I was going across the road from his door, a man gave it me to carry; I went along; the prosecutor came and knocked me down and collared me; he would not go after the man. Witness. He had got across the road when I took him—I first saw him about five yards from the door—this is the cloth.
GEORGE ROBINSON ( police-constable K 370.) I took the prisoner in charge—he said he was carrying it for a man behind—I saw no man behind—I came up about ten minutes after it happened.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2212]
2212. JOHN HOPKINS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, 1 pair of boots, value 3 s. 6 d., the goods of Henry Warrener.
HENRY WARRENER . I am an apprentice to a shoemaker, and live in Strutton-ground—the prisoner lived in the same house. I left a pair of boots in the room he slept in—I missed them afterwards—these produced are them—I had placed them there on the 20th of August.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What was the prisoner in the house? A. A workman—he had been there six or seven weeks—the boots are worth 3 s. 6 d.—people coming into the employment have 1 s. footing to pay.
JOHN JAMES LLOYD . I am a pawnbroker, and live at No. 18, Strutton-ground. I have a pair of boots, pawned by the prisoner for 1 s. 6 d., on the 20th of August, in the after part of the day.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen him before? A. Frequently.
SAMUEL BRYAN . I am a policeman. I took him in charge—he said he knew nothing of it—I found the duplicate in the cell he was locked up in.
JOHN JAMES LLOYD re-examined. This is the duplicate of them.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Three Months.

2213. ALEXANDER WETHERLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of August, 1 plane, value 4 s.; 1 square, value 2 s.; and 1 auger, value 1 s.; the goods of George Cock; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE COCK . I am a carpenter, and live at Putney. On Friday, the 13th of August, I was at work on the top of a building at Putney—I went to dinner at twelve o'clock, leaving some tools on the building—I missed them about a quarter after one, when I came back—they were worth 7 s.—these are them—( produced.)
WILLIAM PARKER ( police-constable T 47.) I was on duty at Chiswick on the 13th of August. I stopped the prisoner in Devonshire-row, with one plane, one square, and an auger—I asked where he got them—he said they were his own property, he had brought them from his own home; that he took them to Hammersmith to pawn, but they refused to take them—I stopped him five miles from Cock's premises, at a quarter to seven o'clock, the same night as the robberying
JOHN JONES ( police-constable T 31.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace of Clerkenwell Sessions—( read)—he is the person—I have known him eleven years.

[trialAccount t18410823-2214]
2214. WILLIAM HALL was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of January, 1 sovereign, 1 half-crown, and 4 shillings; the monies of James Beamont, his master.
JOHN BEAMONT . I am a baker, and live in Sophia-street, Poplar. On the 2nd of January the prisoner was in my service—I gave him a sovereign, a half-crown, and four shillings, to go to Ratcliff Mill for half a sack of flour—I usually have my flour from there—I had sent him there once before—he did not return, but was taken into custody six months afterwards.
GEORGE SWEET . I know the prisoner—I have seen him several times at Ratcliff Mill—at this distance of time I cannot charge my memory whether he came on the 2nd of January or not—I believe he did not—I have no entry of his coming, and I enter all the goods I deliver—I delivered several parcels of flour that day on account of Mr. Beamont, but I have no recollection of seeing the prisoner.
WILLIAM SLADDEN . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner on the 17th of August—I said, "Do you know what I want you for?"—he said, "Oh yes, I have been very uneasy in my mind for the last week; I have been expecting it"—as I brought him to the office, he said he had been sliding, and lost the money, and was afraid to go back to his master, and that he left the barrow at the mill.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not like to go back, because I had lost the money.
MR. BEAMONT re-examined. I had no character with him—he was with me about twelve months—he once stopped away with a little money I gave him to go to town with, but I forgave him that, and he worked it out—the barrow was found at the mill.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410823-2215]
2215. ELIZABETH M'GUMRY was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of August, 90 1/4 yards of printed cotton, value 4 s. 9 d. the goods of William James Stevenson.
JOHN GIBSON BROWN . I am apprentice to William James Stevenson, a linen-draper, in Ratcliff-highway. On Saturday, the 21st of August, I was serving a customer, and saw the prisoner come into the shop, go to the counter, and look at a shawl which laid on it—she took it up—I saw her pull up her dress, and put the shawl so as to screen herself—I called Mr. Brown forwards, he came forward—she asked the price of a shawl, which was 2 s.—she bid 1 s. 6 d., and he went to the other end of the shop, and she went out—I saw she walked with difficulty, and called Brown again—she was fetched back, and as she came into the shop she made a shuffle with her legs—Brown said, "Now I will search you, and see what you have got"—he moved her away, and there was the dress underneath—she had left the shawl on the counter before she went out—I am certain the dress was not on the floor till she came back.
Prisoner. I never laid my hand on any thing on the counter except the shawl—I offered 1 s. 6 d.—he refused, and I went out—when I came
back, I shuffled my clothes, because my petticoat was coming off, but I never had it—he said, "You must have had it about you;" but I had never seen it. Witness. It was on the counter about an hour, or an hour and a half before she came into the shop—I noticed the floor particularly before she came in—there was nothing on the floor at all before she came back—here is a mark on the dress.
JURY. Q. Were you behind the counter? A. Yes, I was behind the opposite counter—the gown-piece laid a yard and a half from where she was—it was found about a yard and a half from the counter.
THOMAS WILLIAM BROWN . I am shopman to Mr. Stevenson. The witness spoke to me—I went to the counter where the prisoner was—she offered 1 s. 6 d. for the shawl—I then moved to the other end of the shop—I showed her another shawl, and said it was much better than the one she was looking at—I observed something rather bulky at the lower part of her person, but I did not like to examine her—I walked further down the shop—the witness called to me to observe her difficulty in walking—she could hardly walk, as she had the dress between her legs—observing her difficulty in walking, I fetched her back—she slipped from my hold—I followed her into the shop, and said, "Now I will see what you have"—she shifted her legs—I said, "Let me see what you have dropped"—I moved her on one side, and there was the dress—she immediately said, "You did not see me take it, though," and said I did it through spite, because she would not buy the shawl—I bad seen the dress on the counter not ten minutes or a quarter of an hour before—the bulky appearance of the lower part of her stomach was gone when the dress was found.
Prisoner. You did not follow me. Witness. Yes, I did, and took hold of you.
JAMES GIFFORD . I am a policeman. The prisoner was given into my custody—at the station the inspector asked where she slept last night—she said she did not know, nor where she slept the night before—she said she did not take the dress.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had the dress in my possession—my own clothes were falling off, and I put my hand down to hold them on.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2216]
2216. WILLIAM SEAL was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering, on the 9th of August, a forged request for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud Lowther Jackson.
LOWTHRR JACKSON . I am a tailor and outfitter, and live in Three Colt-street, Limehouse. On Tuesday, the 9th of August, the prisoner, who is a stranger, came into my shop, looked round, and said, "I think I have got in to the wrong shop"—I asked what he wanted—he said, "A suit of clothes"—I said I could make them as cheap as any other person, and they would be 2 l. 10 s.,—he looked at some things at the door, and said he wanted a pilot-coat, which came to 1 l. 15 s.—my foreman measured him for the cloth—he turned round and asked me to lend him some money, as he had just come home—I lent him 2 s. 6 d.—he said he had come home in the Calcutta ship, and had been a long trading voyage—he came again between eight and nine o'clock at night, and wanted more money—I said I could not give him any till I made inquiry—he pulled a paper out of his pocket, and said, "I have given my captain your card,
and he is going to send four or five more men to supply with clothes—he asked for a black silk handkerchief, which I gave him, and then a cotton one—I said I should send down to his captain next morning, in the West India Dock, to make inquiry; and my young man did go—this is the paper which the prisoner produced to me—I furnished him with two handkerchiefs, a silk and a cotton one, on the credit of that paper, believing it at the time to be true—I said, "Why, you told me you belonged to the Calcutta, and this says the Mary Jane" —he said, "Oh, no, I am bound to Calcutta on a trading voyage"—( paper read)—"Ship Mary Jane. Mr. Jackson,—Please let the bearer have clothes what he wants—for Cook, William Johnson—William Nargot, Captain; owner, Mr. Redman."—he was dressed as if he had just come off a voyage.
Prisoner. I left the West India Dock; I had no money, I had spent it all; I met a man, who said, "My father can let you have goods till you get your pay;" we went to Jackson's; he gave me his card; the man measured me for the coat; I went at night and said, "Lend me a shilling to get a drop of beer?" he said he would give me half-a-crown, which he did; he gave me a card; he said, "If you want money come to me, I will let you have money and clothes;" I had a neck handkerchief and a pocket handkerchief. Witness. I did not supply him with any goods subsequent to the order being presented—before he brought the order I lent him half-a-crown.
Prisoner. I did not give him that paper. Witness. I swear he did—he came in by himself.
HENRY GRIFFITHS . I am traveller to Mr. Jackson. I went to the West India Docks to inquire for the Mary Jane —I saw the prisoner—he said the vessel laid in the dry docks—I said, "Is the captain on board?"—he said "Yes"—I said, "You had better come on board with me"—he said, "No, I have to go on a message, I will come by and by"—I found a vessel of that name at last—I found an old man on board—there was a broom at the mast, which intimated that the vessel was for sale—she had no appearance of having gone a voyage—he said the vessel was lying in the dry docks, but I could not find her there at all.
Prisoner. Q. Who gave you soup at the galley? A. I never had any—he invited me to breakfast in the morning when he gave Mr. Jackson the note;
GEORGE ROBINSON ( police-sergeant I 36.) The prisoner was given into my custody in High-street, Shadwell—I took him to Mr. Jackson's shop—the prisoner asked to look at the paper—Mr. Jackson had not got it with him then.
MARY POWELL . I am the wife of William Powell, and live in Narrow-street, Limehouse. The prisoner came to me, and said he had come from the captain who had just come home in the Mary Jane, and the captain had sent him to lodge at my house—I asked his name—he said, William Seal, and that I was to go with him in the morning to the captain to know what he was to pay, but he was to go and tell the captain what was the charge for his week's board and lodging—I said 14 s. a week—he went away, and came back again—I gave him tea—he went out after tea, and said he was going to buy a few things—he said his ship came from Africa—he came back again with a few things, and said he had paid 6 s. for the black silk handkerchief, and 1 s. 6 d. for the pocket handkerchief, and asked if I thought them dear—I said, "I do not know"—he had supper, and breakfast in the morning, and never paid for it.
Prisoner. The woman is right enough; I do not know any thing about the paper nor about Mr. Jackson; the other man tells the truth.
LOWTHER JACKSON re-examined. He is the man, and he brought that paper—I have listened to all that has been said against me and have nothing to say.
Prisoner. That young man brought the order into the shop.
GUILTY .* Aged 23.— Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18410823-2217]
2217. THOMAS SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of July, 1 saucepan, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Richard Powell: 1 plane, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 kettle, value 6 d. the goods of John Cayzer: and CATHERINE SULLIVAN , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN CAYZER . I am a carpenter, and live in China-place, St. George's. My house stands in a yard by itself—I have a bench and other things in that yard—I came home on Monday night, the 5th of July, a little after twelve o'clock—I found my yard gate open, and the house door also—I sent for my son to fetch a light, and found the prisoner, Thomas Sullivan, under the bench—I pulled him out, and asked what brought him there—he said his father had been beating him, and he came to sleep there—I was going to give him a shaking, but in coming through the passage I stumbled over a stove and a blue dish, and I gave him in charge of a policeman—he at first denied having touched the things in the passage—he afterwards said he had moved them out of the yard to take over to his mother—I missed a saucepan that night—I asked him about it, and a kettle—he said he did not know any thing about it—I missed the plane in the morning—I had used it the evening before at dusk.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Do you leave your gate loose? A. In the day-time, not in the night, it is left so that we know how to undo it, but it is shut so that strangers cannot open it—I have not found the kettle—I cannot prove the prisoner took it, but it was missing at the time.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.—Monday, August 30 th 1841.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2222. GEORGE TATHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of August, 1 necklace, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Joseph Barnett, from the person of Samuel Barnett.
ANN BARNETT . I live with my father, a labourer, in Fleur-de-lis-court, Spitalfields. About five o'clock in the evening of Tuesday, the 17th of August, I was crossing Stewart-street with my brother Samuel in my arms—he is six months old—I felt a snatch, and missed a necklace from his neck—I turned round and saw Mr. Cheesman, and saw the prisoner running—I did not see who did it—Mr. Cheesman ran after him and overtook him—the necklace was produced by Mr. Phillips.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you known the prisoner before? A. No.
ROBERT CHEESMAN . I saw the prisoner go behind the witness, snatch the beads off the child's neck, and run away—I followed him—he ran off and threw them down—I took them up and pursued him till the policeman took him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you want to get any money of any body? A. No—when I left the Magistrate on the 18th, I went to a public-house and had a pint of half-and-half—a man followed me who represented him-self to be related to the prisoner—his father and mother said, "You have no occasion, as your deposition is not taken, to appear again"—I said I must, and a man said, "I will pay your expenses if you will go out of the way"—I inquired of my solicitor, and he said if I took any thing I should be liable to be indicted—I had appointed to meet the prisoner's father (seeing them in such distress) on the Friday evening, at six o'clock, at the sign of the Raven, in Mile-end-road, but when I found that I should be wrong to do it, I sent a person to the father on Friday morning to say I would not meet him—I did net ask 3 s. a day for three weeks from the father—it was mentioned by the father and others of the party—I did not get a farthing.
COURT. Q. Did you know the prisoner before? A. No, I never saw him—on my solemn oath I saw him take the necklace from the child's neck.
MICHAEL CONWAY ( police-constable H 138.) I saw the prisoner running, and took him—Cheesman came to the station-house and said he saw him take the beads from the child's neck—the prisoner did not say any thing.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say nothing? A. I asked him if he had seen any necklace—he said no—when the depositions were taken he said he was very sorry—I do not recollect him saying any thing about the beads in my presence.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined One Month.—The last Week Solitary.
[trialAccount t18410823-2223]
2223. BENJAMIN EVERETT was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 28lbs. weight of coffee, value 2 l. 5 s. and 1 bag, value 6 d.; the goods of John Chatfield.
THOMAS ISRAEL BUNDY . I am servant to Mr. John Chatfield, who lives in Aldgate. On the 12th of August I put this bag of coffee at his door, inside—I saw it safe at ten o'clock, and soon after the street-keeper spoke to me, I then missed it.
GEORGE TEAKLE ( police-sergeant H 8.) I stopped the prisoner with this bag of coffee, in Essex-street, Whitechapel, about eleven o'clock in the morning—there was another roan with him, whom we took, but he made his escape—the prisoner said he received it of that man.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How far is it from the prosecutor's shop to where you took the prisoner? A. About five minutes, walk—the prosecutor's shop could not be seen from there.
NOT GUILTY .

2225. GEORGE HOLLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, 2 gowns, value 7 s.; 1 shirt, value 1 s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 3 d.; and 1 handkerchief, value 3 d.; the goods of Ann Woodman; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ANN WOODMAN. I lodge at Mrs. Hurst's. I found the prisoner in my room about four o'clock in the day, on the 19th of August—he was partly undressed—he had a pair of my stockings on his legs, and my handkerchief in his pocket—Mrs. Hurst had found these gowns and shirt before she called me—they are my property—the prisoner had no business in my room.
MARIA HURST . I live in Went worth-street The prisoner came to lodge in my house on the night of the 18th of August—I called him at nine o'clock the next morning—he got up, came down, and appeared very, ill and very sick—I said, "You don't seem well, would a bason of tea be of any use to you?"—he said, "Yes, and thank you too"—I said, "You had better go and lie down again"—he went up, and laid till four—I then went to him to know if he would get up—he said, "Yes"—I went up again in about ten minutes, and could not see him in his room—I looked in another room, he was not there—I then looked in the further room, and he was standing by Mrs. Woodman's bed-side—I said, "What are you doing?"—he said, "I don't know"—I found this gown in his hat—I said, "You are robbing the woman"—I locked the door, ran down, and called Mrs. Woodman up—when I went up again he was half undressed—he was dressed when I first saw him, and I suppose he had these things on him, as there was nothing on the bed when I went down, and when I came up again some of these things were on the bed, and the rest on him.
PIERCE DRISCOLL ( police-constable H 24.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.

2226. THOMAS M'DONNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of July, 1 coat, value 18 s., the goods of Isaac Joscelyne.
ISAAC JOSCELYNE . I am a tailor, and live in Shoreditch. On the morning of the 10th of July I put a summer coat at my door—I missed it in about an hour—it was brought back by my shopman, and this now produced is it.
ROBERT SCALES . I am a letter-sorter in the Post-office. I was near the prosecutor's door that morning—I saw the prisoner and another lad together—they went to Mr. Joscelyne's shop, and sat down a little while—the prisoner then changed his apron with the other—the other then took the coat off the horse, and the prisoner ran away with it.
Prisoner I was at work at Woolwich that day, seven miles from here.
GUILTY .** Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.

2227. MARTHA CORBETT was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of September, 1840, 10 printed books, value 2 l.; 9 blankets, value 2 l. 5 s.; 2 pairs of pillow-cases, value 6 s.; 1 table-cloth, value 5 s.; 5 sheets, value 2 l. 10 s.; 1 carpet, value 3 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 4 s.; 1 sheet, value 12 s.; 2 towels, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2 pillows, value 10 s.; 2 quilts, value 10 s.; 1 pair of boots, value 9 s.; 1 carpet-cover, value 10 s.; and 4 dishes, value 18 s.; the goods of William Thurnell, her master.
MARY THURNELL . I am the wife of William Thurnell, of Regent-place, City-road. The prisoner was my servant—I searched her boxes, and found nothing—I then searched her person, and found four duplicates of a blanket, a bolster, a pillow, pawned at Mr. Somers's—they are mine—my husband afterwards found about thirty-eight more duplicates in the kitchen.
WILLIAM HUMPHREYS . I live at Mr. Somers's, a pawnbroker. I have a bolster, pillow, and two blankets, pawned by the prisoner.
Prisoner. Mary Ann, their niece, asked me to pawn these things—I did not refuse it, but I gave all the money I received for them to Mary Ann, and she gave it to her sister, who was discharged. On Saturday night, the 14th, my mistress accused me of taking these things, and turned me out of doors at twelve o'clock at night—I told her her niece gave me them, but she would not hear what I had to say, because they were in my name. She owes me a quarter's wages, which is 2 l. 1 s.
MRS. THURNELL re-examined. I have a niece, named Mary Ann Smith—she positively denies every thing of the kind—she was before the Magistrate, but her evidence was not wanted—she is nineteen years old, and has a good character—she has a sister, who is married.
GUILTY . Aged 49.— Confined One Year.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

2232. WILLIAM WELLHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, 1 pair of boots, value 9 d., the goods of George Ruby; and that he had been before convicted of felony.

TIMOTHY WELLS ( police-constable V 83.) I took the prisoner—I asked him if be had stolen the boots—he denied it—in going to the station, he said he had stolen them.
Prisoner. The woman called me back to buy a halfpenny-worth of pears; another boy told me to go and steal an old pair of boots, and put them into the basket.

Prisoner. I am not the person. Witness. Yes, he is the person—he had a red neck handkerchief on—there was another with him.

2234. THOMAS FINNIGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of August, 14lbs. weight of nails, value 3 s., the goods of John Jay, his master.
ROWLAND MORGAN . I live in Hampton-street, Gray's-inn-road, and am a carpenter. The prisoner was employed as a plasterer's labourer for my master, Mr. John Jay—he had no right to have any nails in his business—I cannot swear to these nails, but I had just such—I could not miss any, I had such a number in my care—he had no right to take any away.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were these nails lying about? A. I cannot say that none of them were lying about—I remember the prisoner being employed there about three months.
GEORGE HANBURY ( police-constable D 102.) I stopped the prisoner in the Uxbridge-road, on the 26th of August, with a basket on his shoulder—I asked what he had got—he said, "Chips, and a bottle"—I searched, and found these nails—I asked how he came by them—he said he brought them from where he had been at work, at Mr. Jay's, the builder, and was going to nail a box up—I searched him at the station, and found on him as many more nails as I could hold in my hands—I said next morning that he was very foolish in taking them—he said he picked them up in one room and another.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.

2235. WILLIAM COLLINS was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of June, 1 saw, value 2 s., the goods of Joseph Fletcher.

I lost a saw of my master's about three months ago—I inquired, but could not hear of it—Mr. Dicker showed it me again—this is it— ( looking at it)—I know it by two marks on the handle of it, and a mark on the back.
JAMES RICHARD WATTS . I am servant to Mr. Dicker, the pawnbroker, in Commercial-road. I know the prisoner—I had seen him before—on the 8th of June I took in this saw of him—I am sure he is the person.
Prisoner. I am innocent; I have pledged tools frequently for one Clark; he told me had a chest of tools, and seeing the distress he and his mother were in, I pawned them, not knowing I was doing wrong.

2237. WILLIAM BURNHAM was indicted for embezzlement.
HENRY JOHN KINGHAM . I keep the King's Arms public-house, in Claremont-row, Islington. The prisoner was my pot-boy—it was his duty to take out beer, and to receive money—if he received the monies stated in this indictment he has not paid it to me.
ELIZABETH VILLETT . I took beer of the prisoner—I paid him 6 d. a day for his master up to the 18th of August.

2238. PURSGLOVE LESLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of August, 1 pair of boots, value 5 s., the goods of William Henry Cox.
CATHERINE COX . I am the wife of William Henry Cox, a shoemaker, and live in the City-road. The prisoner occupied a furnished room in our house. On Saturday, the 21st of August, he did not come home at night, and on Sunday morning I missed three pairs of boots, which had been safe in the shop as late as half-past twelve on Saturday night—about three the next afternoon I went up to the prisoner's bed-room, and to my great surprise I found him there—I asked him how he came there—he told me not to be alarmed, that he had got over the Lying-in-Hospital wall, and in at the back parlour window—he went out—I went up to his room, and saw the string of these boots part of the way out of his portmanteau, which stood under the window—I told my husband—he got a policeman—in the evening the prisoner came home, and was accused of it—he said he was guilty, and he meant to return them.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Are these the boots? A. Yes— they are torn by the lace-holes—they have been tried on some one—we rather think the prisoner had a female into the house—he could get one in by opening the door when he was in the house—I think some female must have had these on—no man could have got them on—the prisoner said he was drunk—I said, "I have a charge to make about a pair of drab boots I have missed"—he said, "I know I have taken them, they are in my portmanteau"—he gave the policeman the key, and the boots were there—he had been at home nine or ten weeks—he gets drunk most abominably, and then he is the most troublesome fellow that ever came into a house.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410823-2239]
2239. BARTHOLOMEW HARRINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of August, 2lbs. 14oz. weight of bacon, value 2 s., the goods of James Osborn.
ANN OSBORN . I am the wife of James Osborn—we keep a shop at Walham-green, Fulham. On the 17th of August the prisoner came in, took up this bacon, and looked at it—I kept my eye on him—he turned round, put the bacon under his jacket, and walked out—I followed him, and never lost sight of him till I gave him in charge, with the bacon on him.
Prisoner. Q. Where was I? A. I followed you into Mr. Giles's shop—I had not time to say any thing to you when you took it—you ran out of the shop.
