[trialAccount t18410823-2240]
2240. DANIEL RICKETTS, THOMAS SKIGGS , and WILLIAM BENNETT were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of July, 3 trusses of hay, value 10 s., the goods of Michael Woodhouse.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of James Gingell.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
MICHAEL WOODHOUSE . I gave directions to send two loads of hay to Whitechapel-market—Gingell was my salesman—I have a little boy named Ebenezer Woodhouse—Ricketts was my carter—it was his duty to carry the hay to Whitechapel.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. You are a farmer, and grow hay and clover? A. Yes—my hay-binder is not here—I know nothing about two loads being bound up—Gingell's business is to sell the hay—he pays me the money when I like to go after it—sometimes a month or two passes—he always passes me the bills of parcels back from the man that has it—I do not recollect the price at all—I expect the salesman to do the best he can for me—Ricketts has been in my service sixteen years—he was always honest for any thing I know—he lives in my house as in-door servant.
MR. DOANE. Q. Have you been paid for this hay? A. Yes, by Gingell.
WILLIAM BYERS . I am in the service of Mr. Gingell, a salesman in the hay market, Whitechapel. On the 10th of July, Mr. Banks, the managing-man for Mr. Rhodes, came to purchase two loads of hay—there was a wagon of Mr. Woodhouse's, which had two loads on it—I sold it for two loads, seventy-two trusses—Ricketts and a little boy came with it—I promised to send this hay to Banks's, for Mr. Rhodes—I did not expect to be paid till he received it—Ricketts and the boy went with it—they were to go direct to Mr. Rhodes at Stoke Newington—they had no right to go any where else.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. You know the mode of this business? A. Yes—the hay-carts are driven into High-street, Whitechapel, when a purchaser comes, and we make the bargain—I agreed to sell this for six guineas a load—I told Mr. Banks I could not take less—Rhodes had dealt with me before—they used to pay once in six weeks, or when we liked to take a bill—I cannot tell when they paid for it—it was a goodish bit after it was sold—perhaps a month.
WILLIAM BANKS .—I am foreman to Messrs. Rhodes. I purchased two loads of hay—they were to be delivered before I paid for them—they were to be sent to Stoke Newington. On Saturday morning, the 10th of July, I went and saw some clover—I asked Byers the price, he said, "Six guineas"—I desired him to send two loads to Stoke Newington—there were seventy-two trusses in two loads—I presumed he sent it home—I got home twenty minutes before twelve o'clock—when I got home, I found the wagon and hay had arrived—I saw Searing and Carswell, and in consequence of what Carswell said to me, I went to the station—I got Mr. Pritchard and an officer, and took Ricketts into custody for stealing three trusses of clover—I went with Pritchard to the Elephant public-house, and
saw Pritchard speak to Bennett, the ostler—after going into one place and another, I saw three trusses of clover—I said, "These are three I purchased this morning"—there was other hay of different descriptions— Pritchard asked how it came there—Bennett hemmed and hawed, and then said he purchased it last Thursday—these three trusses were worth 10 s. 6 d.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was not what you said that they were three you thought were yours? A. Yes, they looked like mine—it has turned out since that only two were of the same kind as mine—they are all three of the same quality—I speak from merely seeing it in the cart, and then looking at it in the stable.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. I suppose at Whitechapel, there are public-houses? A. I seldom go into one, except to have a bit of bread and cheese—I cannot tell whether I did that morning—I cannot recollect Byers and I going to a public-house—it is not customary to bind the bargain—I agreed to give six guineas a load.
JOSEPH SEARING . I live at Cheshunt, in Herts. On this Saturday, I was driving a horse and cart—I saw Woodhouse's wagon with two loads of hay—I saw Ricketts and the boy on the top, and Skiggs was driving the wagon—they stopped at the Elephant—Rickett's got off, and went with Skiggs into the house, and had some beer, Skiggs then came and took the fore horse, and Ricketts drove the hind horse, and turned down the street by the stable-door—I saw Bennett by the stable—I saw Skiggs undo the ropes—Ricketts got on the top, and took three trusses down, the first Bennett took in his arms, the next truss Ricketts set down on the ground—then he took the other down, and Ricketts spread the cloth over the remainder of the clover—then Bennett, Ricketts, and the boy came to the door, and Bennett gave Ricketts some money—I do not know how much.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How many times have you been charged with felony? A. Never.
EBENEZER WOODHOUSE . I am nephew of Mr. Woodhouse. I am twelve years old—I came up with two loads of my uncle's hay on this occasion—Ricketts was the carman—after that I went with Ricketts to Mr. Rhodes's—before we got out of Whitechapel, Skiggs came to us—after he came up, Ricketts got on the top of the wagon, and Skiggs drove—they stopped at the Elephant, in the Kingsland-road—we stopped there, and Skiggs took the fore horse, and Ricketts drove the hind horse, and took the wagon down by the side of the public-house stable—I saw Bennett by the side of the stable—Skiggs undid the ropes, and Ricketts put the head of the tilt up, and then put three trusses of hay down—Bennett got them, and put them into the stable—Bennett and Ricketts had some drink, and Skiggs went on with the wagon—Bennett gave Ricketts some money, and then I went on after the wagon.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you all the time? A. On the wagon—Skiggs drove it round into the high road again—I saw Mr. Searing behind with his cart—I did not tell any body about it.
ALLAN CARSWELL . I am in the service of Messrs. Rhodes—when the wagon arrived there were sixty-nine trusses—there ought to have been seventy-two—I remember Mr. Banks returning from the market before the prisoners left the yard—Skiggs was in the yard at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. What are you? A. Assistant in
the yard—I count all the trusses, unless Mr. Bennett is there—the men were given in charge—Mr. Woodhouse saw the hay found at Bennett's.
JAMES DANIELS . I accompanied the inspector to Messrs. Rhodes, and there took Ricketts into custody.
BENJAMIN PRITCHARD . I am an inspector of police—I went with Banks to the Elephant—I saw Bennett, who is ostler there—I asked him to allow me to go into his stable—he made no difficulty—I said I suspected there was hay that had been stolen—he opened the lock, and we found nothing there—he opened another stable—there were three trusses of hay, which Mr. Banks pointed out—there were other trusses of very different sort all together—Mr. Banks said, "How came they here; how long have you had them"—he hesitated, and said, "Oh, on Thursday."
THOMAS RICHARDS ( police-constable N 208.) I took Skiggs—he asked what for, I told him—he said he would make them pay well for his time.
(Bennett received a good character.)
RICKETTS— GUILTY . Aged 42.— Confined Three Months.
SKIGGS— GUILTY . Aged 29.
BENNETT— GUILTY . Aged 32.
Confined Nine Months.
(There was another indictment against Ricketts and Skiggs.)
[trialAccount t18410823-2241]
2241. CHARLES HUGHES and JAMES BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of August, 2 breast-pins and 1 chain, value 5 s., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
CHARLES JACKSON ( police-constable A 146.) On Saturday afternoon, the 7th of August, I saw the two prisoners, in company with two others, near Westminster-Bridge—there was a rowing-match—I saw them walking at the back of different people, up and down the bridge—after watching them some minutes, I saw one, not in custody, put his hand over, and take something from a gentleman's breast, which was immediately handed to Hughes, who was standing on the other side of the way—Brown was covering the man who took the pin—Hughes put something into his pocket—I immediately went over to him, took hold of his arm, and took this pin from his trowsers' pocket—the same pocket he had put the pin into—he had not got his hand out—at the same time another officer came up—I gave Hughes to him, and took Brown—I am sure he was covering the other man—my brother constable and I went to the spot where the pin was stolen from—I held it up, and asked what gentleman in the crowd had lost a pin—one gentleman turned round, and said, "This is my property, drawn from my breast"—there were the marks of the pin in his stock—we asked him to accompany us to the station, but in going we missed him, or he missed us—we have heard nothing of him since.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was there a good crowd of people? A. Yes—Brown stood Behind the other—when we took Hughes, Brown had got eight or nine yards—he was within our sight, and could see us.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you standing when you saw them first? A. On the other side of the way—but when the boats were starting off, I saw them closing on a gentleman—I was half-way across when the pin was handed to Hughes.
JOHN PASMORE MUMFORD ( police-constable A 35.) I saw the prisoners
and two others walking behind the persons who were looking through the balustrades—I saw Jackson cross and take Hughes, and take the pin from the lining of his pocket—I took him, and heard the gentleman say it was his pin—I had seen the prisoners in company before this.
WILLIAM MORRIS ( police-sergeant A 20.) I know the prisoners—they are acquainted—I saw them together on the 26th and 27th of May, at Epsom—that was the first time I had seen Hughes, but Brown I had seen for some years.
HUGHES— GUILTY . Aged 21.
BROWN— GUILTY . Aged 34.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2242]
2242. WILLIAM DOUGLAS was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, 2 trusses of hay, value 5 s., the goods of Thomas Chapman, his master.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS CHAPMAN . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Cheyne-street, Bedford-square—I have some premises in George-street, Euston-square, I keep hay and fodder there. In consequence of what I heard, I counted the trusses of bay, on the night of the 15th of August, and on going on the morning of the 16th I missed two trusses of meadow-hay—the prisoner had been in my service, and had gone out with his cart that morning—I pursued and overtook him near the seventh mile-stone—the policeman stopped him—I went to the cart, and found the two trusses of hay, which are mine—I send food for my horses in the nose-bags, but no hay.
GEORGE MOORE . I am a cow-man, in the prosecutor's employ. On Monday morning, the 16th of August, I saw the prisoner go to the hayloft, take two trusses of hay, and put them into his cart—he went on to Edgeware—I told my master and the policeman of it.
Prisoner. I put them out of the loft, and he put them up to me. Witness. No, I did not.
THOMAS BOYCE ( police-constable S 204,) I went after the cart, at half-past five o'clock that morning, and overtook the prisoner at Edge-ware, and after some distance, I asked him where he was going—he said, "After some green clover"—I said, "Perhaps you will give me a lift"—he said, "Yes, jump up"—I saw in the cart two trusses of hay—I went on with him to the Welsh Harp public-house, where I expected he would have parted with them, but he did not—I then got out, and followed him on—the prosecutor came up, and gave him into custody—I asked how he came by the hay—he said he did not know, but he had been riding on them for two miles.
GUILTY . Aged 37.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2243]
2243. JOSEPH WALTER was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THEODORE MONROE . I live in Harley-street. I am the honorary secretary of the Metropolitan Convalescent Institution, and am a subscriber to it. In September last I was the only secretary to the institution—the prisoner came into the service of the institution about May, 1840—he was to canvas, and collect subscriptions and donations—he was not to keep any subscription or donation longer than a fortnight, and if it amounted to
20 l. or 30 l. he was to pay it in immediately—he was paid by a per centage—he never accounted to me for a subscription from Maria Burrows.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What are you? A. I am in no profession—I am studying, and live with my father—the management of this institution was in a great measure left to me—I was not of age when I first belonged to it—the prisoner accounted to me sometimes at my father's house, and sometimes he had to follow me to the hospital—I had regular books, but I did not take them with me to the hospital—he did not account to any body but me—Dr. Bell belongs to the institution—I left town I think about the last week in July, 1840, and returned about the 26th or 27th of September—I have never, to my knowledge, asked the prisoner about subscriptions which he had accounted for.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When was it you entertained any doubt about his integrity? A. About the beginning of January—I cannot recollect asking him about any subscription previous to that time.
MARIA BURROWS . I am a widow, and live in Albany-street, Regent's-park. On the 30th of September last I paid a guinea to the Metropolitan. Convalescent Institution—I handed it to my servant Dinah Philpott, to give to the person—this is the receipt—it is all written.
DINAH PHILPOTT . I am servant to Mrs. Burrows. On the 30th of September last the prisoner called for a subscription to this institution—I told my mistress, and she gave me a guinea, which I gave him—he wrote this receipt for it—( read.)
DR. WILLIAM BELL . I am a physician, and live in Princes-street; Hanover-square—I am one of the committee of this institution, and a subscriber. The prisoner never accounted to me for a guinea received from Mrs. Burrows—he paid me the money he received during Mr. Monroe's absence, and I gave a faithful account of it to Mr. Monroe.
JURY to MR. MONROE. Q. Has the prisoner acted contrary to the instructions of the committee in giving a written receipt? A. Yes—he had a book of printed receipts, with a margin—I do not remember any instance in which he retained money beyond a fortnight, and then paid it.
NOT GUILTY .—( See page 664.)
[trialAccount t18410823-2244]
2244. RICHARD TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of August, 1 pig, price 6 l., the property of Thomas Flight.
GEORGE FRANCIS . I live in Leicestershire. I brought a white sow from there on the 10th of August, and she was left at Mr. Flight's dairy at Islington—it was of a particular sort, and was going out to New South Wales for breeding—it was worth 6 l.—I saw her safe on the 17th of August—I have since seen a carcass which I supposed to be the same, in New-gatemarket—I have examined it, and have no doubt whatever about it being mine—there was a mark on the near ear.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What was the mark? A. A bit cut out of the under side of the ear—I do not know that there is a more common way of marking pigs, but this was done in a different manner—it was laid upon a board, and cut out in a diamond form—I had no other mark but the peculiar symmetry of the pig—it was between a Chinese and another breed—I do not know how long that breed has been in the market—my father and I have bred them for fifty or sixty years—this pig was lost on the 18th of August—when I lost, it it had its hair on—I saw it again on the 25th—it was then exposed to the air, that might
make it look a little browner—it had been scalded, and its entrails taken out, and the head was taken off—I should think the head had been off a day or two—I did not try to put the head and carcass together.
GEORGE NICHOLLS . I am layer-keeper to Mr. Thomas Flight. I had the keeping of this sow—she broke out on Wednesday fortnight—I have seen the carcass—it is about the size of the one we lost.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you find that she had a notch in both ears? A. Yes.
JOHN HUNT . I saw a white sow wandering near Hollowayturnpike, on Wednesday fortnight—the prisoner said he would take it to Islington-pound —the policeman came up and told him to let it alone, and asked him who it belonged to—he said, "To a cow-dealer in the Liverpool-road"—I saw him take it on as far as Highbury-brewery—he drove it first on one side and then on the other.
WILLIAM PARFETT . On Wednesday fortnight I saw the prisoner in Ball's-pond-road, about half a mile from Mr. Flight's, driving a white sow towards Kingsland-road—it was a very particular kind of sow—it was all white—I cannot say that I had ever seen any of that breed—the sow dropped down twice—I have seen the dead sow—it was somewhat similar to the size of the one I saw the prisoner with.
ROBERT SHINGLES ( police-constable N 104.) The boy Hunt gave me information that he had seen a man driving a sow—I took the prisoner, and asked if he knew any thing about the pig that was lost—he said he did not—I took him to the station—he gave his address at Pearson-street, Hackney-road—I went there and found the pig hanging up at the back of his house—I took it to the station—the same pig has been shown to the prosecutor, and he said he believed it was his.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner gave you his true address? A. Yes, in Nottingham-place, Pearson-street—he said he had some live pigs there, and I found some—I took the dead pig to Laycock's, and hung it up.
JOHN PEGG ( police-sergeant N 19.) The prisoner was brought to the station—I asked him if he had any pigs—he said he had two.
PHEBE BAYLESS . I have known the prisoner two years, on and off, by his killing pigs for me—he bought a poor pig of me in July for 1 l. 17 s. 6 d.—it was a black and white breed, and I slit her ear with a pen-knife—she had a black face and a little white round her eyes—she weighed about seventeen stone—the head of the pig that was found was very much like the one I sold to the prisoner.
GEORGE FRANCIS re-examined. My pig weighed about twenty stone, and the dead one weighed eighteen stone six pounds without the head.
Cross-examined. Q. What would it weigh with the head? A. Nearly twenty stone.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 56.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2245]
2245. JOHN PORTER was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of August, 1 pair of boots, value 9 s. 6 d., the goods of Thomas Steel.
WILLIAM COOK . I am shopman to Thomas Steel, who lives in the Strand. At half-past two o'clock in the afternoon, on the 16th of August, I was in the shop, and saw the prisoner take this pair of boots off a stand, inside the
door—I ran after him—he was stopped by a gentleman—I came up and took them from him.
Prisoner. A young man asked me to hold them—I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and he ran off. Witness. He begged me to let him off, and said he did it through distress.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18410823-2247]
2247. GEORGE WARD was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, 1 scent-bottle, value 10 s. 6 d., the goods of Henry Kebble.
HENRY KEBBLE . I keep a glass-shop in Bedford-street. On the 14th of August the prisoner came and asked for a smelling-bottle or scent-bottle—he was a stranger—I showed him a number—he said there was none would suit him; he would go to the lady and ask if she would allow me to make one for her; he would go and be back in a quarter of an hour—he left, and by his running across the road 1 had suspicion—I looked at the tray, and missed this smelling-bottle—on the Monday following my son found him in Charles-street, Middlesex Hospital—I am sure he is the person—the smelling-bottle was found at Mr. Denny's, in Oxford-street.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What is it worth? A. 10 s. 6 d.—to me it is worth 6 s. 6 d.—my son attends to the business occasionally—I had noticed this before on the same day—nothing particular attracted my attention to it—I handed it to the prisoner with the rest—I did not say I did not know any thing about it till I had asked my son—I asked my son whether he had moved any bottles—this has no stopper—I am aware mine had no stopper—the prisoner was in the shop five or six minutes—he crossed the road—when he got to the other side he ran—I did not pursue—I said I missed the bottle before it was brought to me—directly the prisoner was gone I said, "This boy has boned a bottle"—I pointed out the fellow one to this—I said I had seen it before the prisoner came in—I am quite certain about it—I bought this bottle in October, 1839—I gave 6 s. 6 d. for it—it has been in the shop—it might have been in the window half a dozen times—other persons besides myself sell bottles.
COURT. Q. Is this a scent-bottle? A. Yes, it is—immediately he was gone I cast my eye on the tray, and it was gone.
HENRY HAMILTON . I am in the employ of Mr. Denny, a jeweller and hardwareman. On the 14th of August the prisoner came, with this smelling-bottle, and left it to be repaired—the top was off and was bruised—it would be more valuable when repaired than unrepaired.
Cross-examined. Q. What value do you attach to it at present? A. They are about two guineas a dozen—two years ago they were worth more—I gave 1 s. 6 d. for it after it was repaired, and it was 6 d. for repairing it—it might perhaps fetch 3 s. 6 d.—I do not think it would be worth 10 s. 6 d.—
when the prisoner came in he desired it to be repaired—he wanted a stick in exchange for it, and I said I would give him 1 s. 6 d.
COURT. Q. If you did not think it worth 1 s. 6 d., you would not have given it? A. No.
ARTHUR GORE ( police-constable D 199, examined by MR. BALLANTINE.) Q. Had not the prisoner told you where to find this? A. On his first examination no bottle was produced, and the Magistrate expressed a desire to discharge him—the prisoner said he had such a thing, and said where it was to be found—without that I should have had no clue to it.
COURT. Q. When did you take the prisoner? A. On the 16th—he was taken to Marylebone station—the prosecutor's son stated why he was taken—the prisoner then denied having taken the bottle.
Witness for the Defence.
CATHERINE SMITH . I am in the habit of making door-mats, and hawking them about—I have to walk about the street a great deal—I live in George-alley, Union-court, Holborn. On Saturday fortnight, as I was walking about, I found a smelling-bottle—it was the colour of this smelling-bottle at any rate—I afterwards saw the prisoner go into a smelling. bottle shop, and I thought he might want one—he ran pretty quickly out of the shop, and round the corner—I thought I should not meet him, but I did afterwards—I offered him the smelling-bottle, and he bought it for 1 s.,—it was a bottle of this description.
COURT. Q. Did you ever find a smelling-bottle before? A. No—I do not know the owner of the shop who lost the bottle—I saw the prisoner run out smartly—he asked me my name, and where I lived—I told him—I never heard that this was lost out of a gentleman's shop, till a gentleman and lady came to me last Friday.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 17— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2248]
2248. JAMES YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 2lbs. weight of copper, value 10 d., the goods of Henry Bristowe, in a vessel in a port of entry and discharge.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of John Wilt and others.
JOHN BOYCE . I am a gate-keeper at the London Docks. On the 12th of August I stopped the prisoner coming out of the gate—I suspected all was not right, and asked what he had got—he said, "Nothing"—I said, "I think you have, come to the box," and from his stomach, next his skin, I took these 21bs. weight of copper—he had been employed working on board the John King —John Wilt and others are the owners, and Henry Bristowe is the master—it was in a port of entry and discharge.
Prisoner. I was in liquor. I have a wife and six children; they are starving. Witness. He had 3 s. 6 d. a day.
HENRY BRISTOWE . I am master of that vessel, and the contents of it are under my care—this copper came from there.
GUILTY . Aged 39.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2249]
2249. MARY M'DERMOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of August, 1 waistcoat, value 1 l.; 1 shilling, and 2 sixpences: the property of Mosts Cox; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
MOSES COX . I am in the service of Johnson and Webb; they are brewers, in Bowl-yard. On the 14th of August, between one and two o'clock in the morning, I fell in with the prisoner—I asked her if she knew of a lodging-house, and she took me to No. 10, Church-street—lam married, but I could not get in at my lodgings—the prisoner went to bed with me—I gave her a shilling, and paid for the bed—I undressed and went to bed—I had a shilling, two sixpences, and two or three halfpence in my trowsers' pocket—I had a waistcoat, and that I put on the box—I did not go to sleep—I was not in bed above three quarters of an hour when the prisoner got up—she put on her gown and shawl, went to my pocket, took my money, and my waistcoat as well—I saw her just going out of the door and called to her, but she went off—I saw her on the Sunday evening following in Broad-street—I accused her of robbing me—she said she knew nothing of me, and had not been in the house—the waistcoat produced is mine.
Prisoner. You gave me 1 s. 9 d., and said you bad no more money—I told you I was not going to stay with you for that, and then we came down stairs.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX . I am a policeman. On Sunday night I received the prisoner from the prosecutor—she told him if he would give her 4 s. she would give him the waistcoat—he refused—I took her to the station—she then said, if he would forgive her she would give the waistcoat—she then said it was at Biddy Ryan's—I went there, and found it between the sacking and the bed.
Prisoner. I said, if he would give me the rest of the 4 s. that he said he would give me, I would get it him; he took me to a house, and sent for a pot of porter, and sent the boy for a policeman.
ANN CLARK . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I searched the prisoner at that time, and was present at her trial—she is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Fourteen Years.

2250. ELIZABETH DUNKS was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of August, 1 shirt, value 3 s.; 2 sheets, value 5 s.; and 1 apron, value 1 s.; the goods of John Constantine Lombardy; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
DOROTHY LOMBARDY . I am the wife of John Constantine Lombardy—we live in Ray-street. I have known the prisoner some years—she acted as my servant off and on—I left home on the 4th of August, and returned on the 14th—the prisoner was in and out at my place during that time— when I returned I missed the articles stated—the prisoner was at her own lodging—I suspected her, and I gave notice to the officer—he found this shirt and two sheets of mine—this is all I have got.
WILLIAM ALEXANDER CHAPMAN . I am a pawnbroker, and live in Clerkenwell. I have a shirt and two sheets—they were pawned with me, but not by the prisoner—this is the duplicate of them.
CHARLES ELLIOTT ( police-constable G 132.) I beard of this, and saw the prisoner coming down Turnmill-street on the 18th of August—I told her I wanted her for a robbery in Ray-street—she said she did not do it—the next day she said she was induced by another girl to steal the property, and gave it to her to pledge—I found the duplicate on the prisoner, and the articles are pawned in her name.
Prisoner. I did not pawn them, nor take them; it was another person; the duplicate was not found on me.
WILLIAM GRAY ( police-sergeant G 12.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Ten Years.

2251. JOHN BURTON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of August, 4 sovereigns and 2 half-sovereigns, the moneys of Ruth Dring.
FRANCIS DRING . I live at Limehouse, and am in the milk line. The prisoner lived five months with me—he had to go about with milk—I left him at home, on the 15th of August, to mind the house while I went to chapel.
RUTH DRING . I am the daughter of Francis Dring. On the 15th of August the prisoner was at home—I had a drawer, in which I had the money stated—it was safe on the Thursday before—there were four sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, and 15 s. 6 d. in silver—I missed the gold—the silver was not taken—I suspected the prisoner, and asked him whether he had been to my drawer—he said no, he had not—I told the policeman—some of the money has been found, and the prisoner owned to it at the station.
Prisoner. They frightened me; I did not know what I was saying.
DANIEL DONOVAN ( police-constable K 319.) The prosecutrix told me of this—I asked the prisoner about it—he said he knew nothing of it—I took him to the station, and found on him a key and a sixpence—I went to his master's, and found the key would not fit the drawer that the money was taken from.
CHARLES WHITE ( police-constable K 259.) I was in the station—the prisoner said, "I have taken the money; three sovereigns are in my box, and two I have spent"—I then went to No. 4, Waterloo-street, broke a box open, and found three sovereigns in it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 16.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.

2252. WILLIAM DE LACY was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of July, 1 plane, value 4 s.; 1 saw, value 3 s.,; 1 square, value 2 s. 6 d.; and 1 bevil, value 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of Robert Cox; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ROBERT COX . I am a carpenter, and work for Mr. Archbutt, of Sloane-street —his shop is in Pelham-place, Brompton. I and some of my fellow workmen left some tools there on the 22nd of July—I locked the door and bolted the windows, and left at seven o'clock in the evening—the next morning, at half-past six, I found some person had got in—I missed the tools stated in the indictment—these produced are them—four of the other men lost tools also.
JOSEPH BAKER . I am a policeman. On the morning of the 23rd of July I fell in with the prisoner about a quarter of a mile from the work-shop, at a quarter-past four o'clock—he was carrying a basket full of tools—he asked me which was the way to Piccadilly, and said he was going to work—he had an apron tied round him like a carpenter, and had a Petersham coat on—I took him, and found these tools.
WILLIAM WALLIS ( police-constable E 64.) I produce a certificate of
the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office— ( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Ten Years.

2253. WILLIAM NEWBERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 1 pocket-book, value 1 s., and an order for the payment of 6 l. 2 s. 6 d.; the property of William Lassiter.
WILLIAM LASSITER . I am porter to Mr. Leslie, a coach auctioneer. On the 9th of August there was a sale going on in Worship-street—I put my coat into a cupboard there, about nine o'clock in the morning—the cupboard had no lock—about four in the afternoon I missed my pocket-book and a cheque from the pocket—I sent a man to stop the cheque—I have never found the hook—this is the cheque for 6 l. 2 s. 6 d.—it was drawn by Mr. Leslie's son.
GEORGE BOLTON . I keep a coffee-house. The prisoner lodged with me for thirteen nights—I asked him, on the 6th of August, for the payment of his lodging, and on the Monday, the 9th, he produced this cheque from a pocket-book similar to what the prosecutor says he lost—he asked me if I could give him the difference—I said I had not got it, hut I would give it him in the morning—the next morning I went to the banker's, and they told me the cheque was stopped, having been stolen the day before—I went home—the prisoner did not come again till the 16th, and then I had him taken.
EDWARD BARKER ( police-constable E 124.) I was called to take the prisoner—he said he picked up the cheque in Newgate-street.
Prisoner. I picked it up in Newgate-street.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Six Months.

2254. JOHN HARRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of August, 46 yards of fustian, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of Benjamin Parnham.
JOHN LACK . On the 20th of August I saw the prisoner in company with another person—the prisoner had the fustian on his shoulder—the prosecutrix came running after them—the prisoner and the other boy ran round Wellclose-square together—the prisoner carried the fustian till they got to Neptune-street, and then gave it to the other—a carman found it.
CATHERINE PARNHAM . I am the wife of Benjamin Parnham—we keep an outfitting-warehouse for seamen, in Grace's-alley, Well-street. This fustian is our property—I saw the prisoner go out of our shop, with the fustian on his shoulders—I followed him and the other to the middle of Wellclose-square—I then returned and called my sister to mind my shop—I then went to the corner of Nortonpassage—there was a crowd there, and a carman went up a passage, and brought this fustian to me—the prisoner was not taken till last Thursday.
GUILTY .* Aged 18— Transported for Seven Years.

2255. MARY BERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of August, 1 kettle, value 10 s., the goods of George Daw.
GEORGE DAW . I am a pastry-cook, and live in Great Marylebone-street. The prisoner used to come charing at our house—some time ago I missed a new kettle—I suspected her, and found it at the pawnbroker's—this now produced is it.
GEORGE ROGERS ( police-constable D 71.) I took the prisoner, and found the kettle.
HENRY WINCH . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this kettle—it was pawned by the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor's wife gave me time to get some things out, which I pawned; I had forgotten this; if they had given me longer time I should have thought of this.
(The prisoner received a good character.)

[trialAccount t18410823-2256]
2256. ROBERT WILKINSON was indicted for feloniously receiving of an evil-disposed person, on the 24th of July, 1 wooden box, value 6 d.; and 289 yards of ribbon, value 5 l. 2 s.; the goods of Daniel Stubbs; well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
DANIEL STUBBS . I am a linen-draper and haberdasher, and live in Church-street, Hackney. On Friday, the 16th of July, I had some boxes of ribbon on the counter—there was a blue box of ribbon, and also a white one—there was a common sort of ribbon in the blue box, which was soiled, remnants and short lengths—in the white box there was this description of ribbon now produced—a great many of them had never been cut, but some were shorter lengths—I was in the shop early on the morning of the 17th, when two men came in—I did not see the ribbons in the morning—I did not notice the boxes—the ribbons were missed on that Saturday—on the following Saturday I was passing along Shoreditch, and in the prisoner's shop window I saw a blue box full of ribbons, which I recognised as mine—I called a policeman, and we went into the shop—I saw the prisoner there, and asked to look at the ribbons in the window—he showed me two boxes of ribbons, before I asked to see the blue one—when he produced that, I took up one of the pieces, looked at it, and found my private mark on the card, inside the ribbon, partly erased—I told the prisoner they were my ribbons, and asked where he bought them—he said he bought them of Cheaper and Co., of Wood-street, three months before, and he could show the invoice—he went and fetched some invoices—the policeman looked over part of them, but could not find any invoice to answer to that—I did not notice the date of them—he was then given into custody—the policeman took possession of the blue box and ribbons—this produced is it—a few days after the prisoner had been taken the policeman showed me some other ribbons, among which I recognised some of mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How soon after the first lot was found did you see the second? A. I do not know to a day, it was two or three days—the box was in the prisoner's window, as well as the ribbons exposed for sale—any one passing could see them—I did not look over the invoices myself at that time—I did a few days afterwards, when the prisoner was in custody.
MR. CHAMBLRS. Q. Who showed you the invoices when you looked
over them a few days afterwards? A. The prisoner's wife—I did not see any thing at all then—I did not stop above a few minutes in the house.
SAMUEL STUBBS . I am the prosecutor's son. I was in the shop on Saturday, the 17th July, when two men came in—one of them went away rather abruptly, and I afterwards missed two boxes of ribbon—I have looked at the ribbons produced, and they are all my father's—I covered this box myself—after the prisoner was in custody, I went to his house with the officer and found about five pieces more of my father's ribbon—they were the same pattern as those we lost—I do not find any of my father's marks on these five pieces—I only judge from the pattern—they are worth about 15 s.
THOMAS PARKER . I am in the house of Cheaper and Co., Wood-street, Cheapside—they used to serve the prisoner with ribbons. I only know from our books when we last supplied the prisoner with ribbons—I have the day-book here in which the goods are entered—we have a ledger as well, in which we book the amount of the different entries—the first entry is made in the day-book.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Is that in your handwriting? A. No, but my name is at the bottom as having given the parcel in.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. When was the last time you sold him ribbons? A. On the 21st of April, 1840.
Cross-examined. Q. I dare say yon will undertake to swear that there were no ribbons sold to him since 1840? A. I will—there are about three of us who sell ribbons—this book is finished—there is another book succeeding this, but that is not here—this book goes down to May, 1841—we are still carrying on business.
MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Have you searched through the other book? A. No, because we have not done any business with the prisoner since 1840, I know that of my own knowledge—the other book begins with 1841—we discontinued our account with the prisoner in 1840, and have not done any further business—ours is a wholesale-house—three of us manage the business, Mr. Jackson, Mr. Arnold, and myself—I see most of the goods that go out—I can fetch the other book.
WILLIAM HOLLAND . I am a policeman. I went into the prisoner's shop with the prosecutor and another officer—some ribbons were shown to the prosecutor—the prisoner produced a file of invoices—I looked them over—I did not take them away—the prisoner said be had bought those ribbons of Cheaper and Co., 36, Wood-street, Cheapside, some months ago—I turned round to the prosecutor, and said, "This is a serious charge to make against an apparently respectable man, look at your ribbons again, and be satisfied beyond the possibility of all doubt, and be sure they are your a"—he looked again and said, "I am sure they are mine, they were in my shop a few days ago, my son papered the box, and previous to being papered with blue there was grey paper"—I looked, and there was grey paper underneath.
HENRY WILLIAM DUBOIS . I am a policeman. I went to the prisoner's shop after he was in custody, and found five pieces of ribbon in one of the drawers behind the counter, which the prosecutor has seen—Hayward, a constable, was there at the time—the prosecutor's son recognized them.
DANIEL STUBBS re-examined. All the ribbons in this box are mine—they are not packed as they were when they were lost—there were more in quantity than are here, and some were in a different box.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did yon not see some of your ribbons in another shop window? A. No—I saw the same patterns in one or two win. dows—I did not go in—the prisoner's shop is about two miles from mine—he lives in Shoreditch—the ribbons were all lost the same day—most of them were in the white box, which is not here—here are one or two pieces in this box which were in it when lost—the others were not found.
WILLIAM HOLLAND re-examined I took away a large quantity of property from the shop in the first instance, which was not claimed—I took away 580 rolls of ribbon, and left about 2000 rolls behind—those produced formed a very small portion of the stock—the prisoner and his wife and a little boy served in the shop—the business was not on a very small scale.
HENRY WILLIAM DUBOIS re-examined. The prisoner, his wife, and a boy, were the only persons I saw in the shop—I took all the books away—I gave two up by application from counsel to the Magistrate—they were small books—the one I retained is not respecting this case—the other two were for entering goods, but I could not make out how they were kept—there was an account of ribbons and various other articles in them, but they were not regularly kept, and there were no dates.
THOMAS PARKER re-examined. I have now brought the other book—there is no entry of any later dealing for ribbons—there are other transacttions since—the last transaction is on the 11th of May, but then he did not buy any ribbons—he has not bought any ribbons since 1840—the last dealing for ribbons was on the 26th of April, 1840—the dealing ceased in May, 1840—there are no dealings entered in the new book at all—I have looked, and there are no dealings later than I have stated.
JURY. Q. Do you ever sell ribbons of different lengths and remnants? A. No—we always sell in half pieces.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18410823-2257]
2257. ROBERT WILKINSON was again indicted for feloniously receiving of an evil-disposed person, on the 24th of July, 47 pots of ointment called nutritive cream, value 4 l. 2 s. 6 d.; 36 pots of bear's grease, value 2 l. 14 s.; 10 bottles of balm of Columbia, value 1 l.; 14 cakes of soap, value 9 s. 4 d.; 24 pots of pomatum, value 7 s.; 3 boxes of tooth powder, value 6 s. 4 d.; 1lb. 14ozs. weight of pins, value 1 s.; 1lb. weight of pomatum, value 2 s.; and 6 pomatum pots, value 1 s. 9 d.; the goods of Thomas Pickford and another, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN GOODMAN . I am in the employ of Messrs. Kennaby, wholesale perfumers, of Westmoreland buildings, Aldersgate-street. On the 24th of July I received an order from Mrs. Scott, of Barnsley, for a number of articles of perfumery—I packed them in a box on the 20th—I have a copy of the invoice which went with the goods—there were two dozen of Finnegan's cream, at 3 s. 6 d.; two dozen ditto, at 2 s. 6 d.; three dozen of Atkinson's bear's grease, at 2 s. 6 d.; one dozen of balm of Columbia, and other articles—among the things now produced are two out of half a dozen of almond shaving cakes; ten out of a dozen balm of Columbia; one dozen and eleven out of two dozen half-size of Finnegan's cream; the whole of the 3 s. 6 d. size; three out of one dozen of don to tooth-powder; and also some soap—the gross amount of the articles invoiced is 14 l. 4 s. 3 d.—the invoiced price of the articles found is about 9 l. 4 s. 2 d.—here are all the
dummy pots which were among the things sent—a number of articles have been produced to me by Hay ward the officer, which in every respect correspond with the articles I had put into the parcel—I know some of them by the mark on the outside wrapper—this "2 s. 6 d." is pat on by Mr. James Kennaby to distinguish the size—that is in precisely the same state as when we sent it out—here is one of the 3 s. 6 d. since, which is packed precisely as it was when I sent it—after packing the articles I put on the address on the box, "Mrs. Scott, Barnsley, Yorkshire"—I then handed the box over to the porter, and directed him to take it to Mr. Pickford's.
JAMES LEWIS KENNABY . This parcel has my handwriting on it—I remember receiving the order for these goods, and calling it over with Mr. Goodman—I remember the packing up of the goods—I know the articles were contained in that package—Finnegan'a cream it an article not very easy to be had—there are but few agents in London for the tale of it—I know this particular article came from our shop—we have had but nine dozen of that size since we commenced dealing with Mr. Finnegan, and I know bow they have all been disposed of with the exception of this portion—all those I have sold went into the country, and none in London at all.
ALEXANDER HOWARD . On the 20th of July I took a package directed to Mrs. Scott, of Barnsley, to Messrs. Pickford's, at the Castle, in Wood-street.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Why do you call it Messrs. Pickford's? A. It is called so—I delivered the goods at the Castle inn to the person who signed my book—that is all I know.
JOHN AYRES . I am a checker off and porter at Messrs. Pickford's, at the Castle inn, in Wood-street. I received a box in the evening part of the 20th of July, and checked it on the delivery book—it was my business to take the box, and place it among the other goods to go to Barnsley, in Yorkshire, by railway—it would leave our place by wagon, which was to go to the Birmingham railway, where we have a warehouse.
WILLIAM HANDS . I am clerk at the Castle inn, in Wood-street—it is the concern of Thomas Pickford and Joseph Baxendale. I remember teeing a package there on the 20th of July, directed to Mrs. Scott, of Barnsley—I attended the loading of the wagon—it was loaded to go to the warehouse at the railway station, at Caraden-town—this package was placed on the wagon soon after it was weighed—I saw it weighed, and checked it off in my book.
Cross-examined. Q. Does Mr. Thomas Pickford interfere in the business at all? A. Yea—I believe he acts there—I know he is in the concern—I heard it from them that know—I have not seen him for some time—I cannot say how long—I cannot tell whether I have seen him within this twelve or eighteen months—I might have seen him within the three years—he is at Manchester—I do not know him, no further than by sight—I saw him once when he was in London—I should know him again if I saw him—he is not tall—he is rather stout—it was about fourteen months or two years ago that I saw him—he was then in the yard.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You have always been the clerk of Thomas Pickford and Joseph Baxendale? A. Yes—they pay me my wages.
GEORGE BASS . I am in the employ of Pickford and Co., and am guard to the wagons that go from Wood-street to Camden-town. On the 20th of July—I took the wagon away from the Castle inn to Camden-town, and saw it unloaded at the warehouse—I do not know whether.
the parcel in question was on the wagon or not—whatever was in the wagon I took to Camden-town—it was not my business to make the way bill—it was Eyre's business to load the wagon.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you see Mr. Pickford? A. I never saw him in my life.
THOMAS PARSONS . It is my place to check the goods that arrive it Pickford's warehouse, at Camden-town station, by the wagon from Wood-street—I am in the employ of Messrs. Pickford and Baxendale.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ever see Mr. Pickford? A. Yes, about eighteen months ago, but not at all since—lam not aware that he has been in London since—I saw him then in Camden-town in a gig with Mr. Bax-endale—I had never seen him before—I was told it was Mr. Pickford by his servants, who have known him for many years—he was a tallish man—he was not old or young—I cannot exactly tell the size of a man sitting in a gig—I have known the firm for these twenty years, and always knew it to go by the name of Pickford and Co.
MARY ANN SCOTT . I live at Barnsley. I gave an order for a quantity of perfumery to Messrs. Kennaby—I never received the goods according to that order.
WILLIAM HOLLAND . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody on Saturday, the 24th of July—when the prisoner was in custody, I and Hay ward went to his house—we had left Fisher, an officer, in the house when we first took the prisoner, and when we returned I saw Hay-ward find, in the parlour, on the left hand side of the door, seventy-three packages, consisting of those now produced—I also saw Hayward bring down twenty-eight more from up stairs, but did not see him find them.
JAMES HAYWARD . I am a policeman. I went back, after the prisoner was in custody, and found Fisher in possession there—I searched the bouse in company with Holland—in the attic, at the top of the house, I found twenty-eight pots of pomatum laid in a large empty box, with some straw at the bottom—the box had no lid to it—I then went down into the parlour at the back of the shop, and on the left-hand side of the door I found seventy-three more packages of perfumery, covered with a handkerchief—I took all the articles to the station, and have now produced them—they—have been seen by Mr. Goodman and Mr. Kennaby.
THOMAS FISHER . I am a policeman. I was left in charge of the premises from the time the prisoner was taken into custody by Holland till they were afterwards searched in my presence by Holland and Hayward, when the prisoner was in custody—I know where these things were found in the parlour—nothing was done with those articles after the prisoner was taken into custody—no one had access to that part of the premises while I was there—they must have been in the same position as when he left—I saw a parcel in the corner, with a handkerchief covered over them, when I first went in, and it afterwards turned out to be the seventy-three articles of perfumery.
Cross-examined. Q. He had been taken away in custody? A. Yes and I remained behind till the officers returned—I was only there about an hour.
HENRY WILLIAM DUBOIS . I am a policeman. I found this book lying on the window-ledge in the parlour at the back of the shop— among numerous other entries there is an entry of "Job of scents 5 l."
Cross-examined. Q. A great many of the other entries are in pencil?
A. A good many—there is no other entry in ink but that, in that page, only the entry in the cash balance—here is another entry in ink on another folio, "Three dozen sticks," and that is the only other one in ink; all the others are in pencil—I took two other books away—the prisoner was in custody at the time—ink marks are much more difficult to rub out than pencil.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. There is a mode of tearing them all off together? A. Certainly, or scratching out with a knife; but I see no erasure here, nor any marks rubbed out—the prisoner was apprehended on the 24th—I found this book on the 26th.
DANIEL BAILEY . I am in the employ of Messrs. Thomas Pickford and Joseph Baxendale. They are the carriers from the Castle inn, in Wood-street, to the station at Camden-town.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you see Mr. Pickford? A. About six months ago—I saw him on our premises then—I have been in the employ ever since—I will not swear I have seen him since.
COURT. Q. You have no reason to suppose he has turned over his business to any one else? A. Not the slightest.
(Henry Bakewell, Church-street, Shoreditch; Charles Theodore Curley, warehouseman to Williams and Mottram, Wood-street; and Samuel Rolfe, tailor and draper; deposed to the prisoner's good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Ten Yean.

2258. JOHN JAMES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, 1 packing-case, value 7 s.; 6 glass bottles, value it. 6 d.; and 6 quarts of wine, value 12.; the goods of John Rockley, his master.
JOHN ROCKLEY. I am a wine-merchant, and keep the New Hummums, hotel, Covent-garden; the prisoner was three or four months in my service, and boarded in the house. On Friday, the 27th of August, I went to the police-station at Bow-street, in consequence of information I received, and there saw a case and some bottles of wine, in consequence of which I gave the prisoner into custody—I can speak to the wine, it being sealed with my name, and the case.
THOMAS ELLIS . I am an oyster-dealer, and live in Bedford-court, Covent-garden. On the 29th of August the prisoner left a box at my door, and I took it in charge—he told me he would bring a horse and cart and take it away, but he did not, and I gave it up to the police—this is the case, I can swear it.
RICHARD GABRIEL GABB . I am a police-sergeant I went to Ellis's shop—he delivered me a case containing this wine, as it does now—I found Mr. Rockley's name on one of the seals.
ALLEN PHILLIPS . I am a policeman. I went to Mr. Rockley's on the 27th, and heard a conversation between the prisoner and Mr. Rockley—the prisoner said "Have mercy on me, master; do not give me into custody"—Mr. Rockley gave him in charge, and on the road to the station, he said he took the wine to send to his wife, for her to make a little money, as she was very badly off and ill in the country—I found two directions on him—they are directions to his wife—I told him what he said I should give in evidence.
Prisoner. The wine I had from a person who used to live at Liverpool with a Mr. Hanson, he is now agent for somebody—he owed me 30 s., and said he would give me the wine for it—it was never on Mr. Rockley's premises,
but the case I took from Mr. Rockley intending to return it—I sealed the cork with Mr. Rockley's seal for my wife to know where I lived.
MR. ROCKLEY re-examined. Some of this wine is Lisbon, which he bottled in my cellar the day before—he had the care of the seal, and of the cellars—I am certain these formed part of my stock—this Hock I know—I have no question but this case was secreted to take out next morning while I was at breakfast.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Seven Years.

2264. JOSEPH WALTER was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
MARIA BURROWS . I am a widow, living in Albany-street, Regent's-park, I am subscriber to the Metropolitan Convalescent Institution—last September I paid the prisoner my subscription for that year—on the 9th of July, my servant, Dinah, came to me for another subscription for this year—I went with her into the dining-room—I saw the prisoner—he said he called for the subscription to the Metropolitan Convalescent Institution—I said it was a very short year—he looked at something, and said it was within a day, either before or after the time that I had paid the subscription last year—I offered him a 5 l. note, which he could not change—I borrowed a sovereign of Dinah—I added 1 s., and gave it him—he wrote this receipt ( read)—I looked next day to my former receipt, and I found the last was paid in September—if I had known the prisoner had been discharged, and was not a collector of subscriptions, I should not have paid him the guinea in July.
DINAH PHILPOTT . On Friday, the 9th of July, the prisoner came—he said he had called from the Metropolitan Convalescent Institution for Mrs. Burrows' subscription—I went to my mistress—she sent me down for a card or paper—I lent my mistress a sovereign—she paid it him—the prisoner gave me a printed paper, which I gave him again.
THEODORE MUNRO . I am honorary secretary to the Metropolitan Convalescent
Institution. I live in Harley-street—the prisoner was discharged from being a collector to the institution in March or April this year—he was collector before that—Mr. Linny was appointed in the prisoner's stead—the prisoner has not paid this guinea to the society, nor had he any authority to receive it, as he had ceased to be a collector of subscriptions.
Cross-examined by MR. ESPINASSE. Q. Is Mr. Linny here to-day? A. Yes—I do not think Linny employed the prisoner to collect subscriptions—I believe Linny did employ him for something.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18410823-2266]
2266. JOHN MORGAN was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SANKEY . I am in the service of Mr. Chancellor, as an omnibus driver from Kensington to Whitechapel. I became acquainted with the prisoner about eighteen months ago—he was in the habit of riding up and down, and sitting by me on the box—I said to him, I should like to buy an omnibus—I had a little money in the Bank—he came to me on the 28th of October—he rode down to Kensington—he gave me half a dozen cards to distribute, and said he had taken a public-house at last, called the White Bear, at Ratcliff-cross—he had been looking for a business for some time—he came to me two days after, and said he wanted to speak to me—he said, "You have got a little money in the Bank; I have got 700 l.; I want you to let me have your 100 l. out of the Bank; I will deposit the lease of my house in your bands as security for it—he said he wanted it to purchase the lease of his house, that his brother and sister were in the house, and he had possession, and was going on Saturday to get the lease; he would procure the lease on Saturday, and deposit it with me—I said, if he would deposit the lease in my hands as a security I would let him have it—I met him, and went to my broker, and sold out 100 l. stock, and got 90 l. in notes, six sovereigns, and six shillings—I walked down towards Whitechapel, and saw the prisoner, and gave him nine 10 l. notes, six sovereigns, and fifteen shillings, in the presence of Mr. Collier—he was" outside the bar—I was induced to part with my money because he said be would bring me the lease up in the evening—I should not have parted with my money if be had not told me he had 700 l., and was in possession of the house—I saw him again on the fallowing Wednesday—Mr. Thompson was with me—we went into a public-house—we saw the prisoner there—I said, "You have got no public-house, where is my money?"—he said, "Do your best, and do your worst," and away he went—I could get no policeman to take him—my friend went and spoke to him—he said, "He may do his best, and do his worst, he shant have a d—d shilling"—I looked for him afterwards every day, and my answer was, he was gone into the country—I found him on the 22nd of last month—I saw him on the Sunday morning after I gave him the money—he said, "Sankey, I have not taken that house; come over the way to-morrow morning, to my uncle Tupcott's house, and I will give you the money"—I went, and saw his wife—she said he was gone into the country—I never could see any more of him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you any money transaction with him before this? A. Never but once—I lent him 15 l. I was to have had 20 l., but eighteen of us sat down to supper—he had it Dearly three months—we were intimate nearly all that time—I did not know he had been a valet in the service of a nobleman—he always stated he had got plenty of property—I did not complain to him of the small interest I got in the funds—I went down to the White Bear public-house at Ratcliff—I found he was in treaty for that house.
WILLIAM COLLIER . I went with the prosecutor to the Grapes public-house in October last—I saw him hand over to the prisoner nine 10 l. notes, six sovereigns, and some silver—Sankey said to the prisoner, "You will let me have that lease"—he said, "Yes, certainly."
ANDREW THOMPSON . I was in company with the prosecutor in October, at a public-house in Leman-street—some conversation took place with the prisoner and the prosecutor—I did not hear any thing they said—the prisoner called me on one side, and told me that Sankey had no witnesses, and therefore he would never pay him a single shilling, and he could not get a single shilling of it.
JOHN LUCKER . I have known the prisoner twelve months—he was a policeman, and then a gentleman's servant—he used to frequent the skittie-grounds very much—he did not appear to be a respectable person—I do not believe he is worth 700 l.—he did not appear worth 300 l.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. The son of a deceased licensed victualler—I am not in the habit of frequenting skittle-grounds.
THOMAS FAREY . I keep the White Bear public-house, Ratcliff-cross. I took it on the 25th of January last, from Charles Holben—he had it about six months—Holben was there in October—I do not know my thing about the prisoner keeping the house—when I took this house its valuation was 100 l.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see any thing of the house in October? A. No—it was to draw four butts of beer a month—it was rather overrated—the lease was 380 l.—it was shut up when I took it.
ANDREW THOMPSON re-examined. Q. I knew this house in October—the prisoner was not in possession.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Seven Years.

2269. JOHN CLIFFORD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
WILLIAM MOORE ( police-constable N 226.) About a quarter before six o'clock, on the evening of the 9th of August, I was called to Pocoek's Fields, Islington—I found a great mob, and two men fighting—I took one of them into custody—the prisoner came and caught hold of me, and with the assistance of the mob got him from me—I pursued the man, and the prisoner came in front of me, and threw me down—I took him into custody, and was taking him to the station—he called to the mob not to let him go—they threw me down, dragged me into the field, and gave me several blows—I got my truncheon out—the prisoner drew a knife, and cut
the cord which held my truncheon, and in cutting the cord he cut my wrist.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you see any knife in his band? A. Yes, and I saw him put his hand into his pocket—I stated that before the Magistrate—I do not know whether it was a prize fight—two respectable men were going by—they told me there was a fight—I was knocked down—I was rolled down on the field by the mob—they gave me several blows in the sides, with which I was laid up for six days—I can produce a certificate from the doctor that attended me—I went to the Magistrate on the day following—I did not take a boy into custody for a row, not for six days after.
Q. Did you ever state the mob were going to drown you? A. Yes, actually they were—I did not go to the Secretary of State to get them to prosecute nor to the Commissioners—I went into the ring, and took one of the men who were fighting—there was no row before that—I cried out, and a good while after I drew my staff—I took it out to use it—they cut the string, and took it off, and threw it out into the hedge—the prisoner cried out to them to assist him to throw me into the water—it was the prisoner who threw me down.
WILLIAM FISHER . I was coming across the fields, and saw the constable come up and take one man—they hustled, and got him away—I saw the prisoner come up, catch hold of the constable by the waist, and be held him till the man got away—I did not see any blows struck.
WILLIAM WHITE . I saw the prisoner wrest the officer's staff away, and throw it over the hedge.
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Judgment Respited.

2271. JOSEPH WALTON was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Joseph Axon, on the 2nd of July, and stabbing and wounding him, in and upon the right thigh, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH AXON . I am a pork-butcher, and live at Romford, in Essex. On the 2nd of July I was at Fairlop fair—I was returning with my brother Thomas, about a quarter-past ten o'clock in the evening, and when we got about a quarter of a mile from the fair I saw a small party collected together, fighting—a person named Cutts was stripped, ready to fight—I spoke him, and after I spoke to him he went on fighting with the prisoner—they both fell, and when they were down the prisoner's wife came and kicked Cutts—I said it was unfair, and my brother went and took the prisoner's wife away, picked the prisoner up, and afterwards picked up Cutts—the prisoner immediately came to me, and stabbed me twice in the back Part of my thigh, with some sharp instrument—I felt the blow, and felt
behind, and felt the blood in my trowsers—I had not interfered in the fight at all—I he stab did not disable me at the time—about five minutes after the prisoner stabbed me, he came to me and hit me with his fist in the forehead—my brother came up, laid hold of him, and I did so also—I let him go—I sent for Mr. Bowers, a surgeon at Romford, as soon as I got home—I was obliged to go to bed, and it was five or six weeks before I was able to do any kind of work—I was under the doctor's care about that time—I was confined to my bed seven or eight days.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you dined to-day? A. That is my business—I have had my dinner, of course—I believe I have had a drop of porter—nothing else that I know of—I am quite sober—I had been in the fair about four or five hours, amusing myself—I had only been in one booth—my brother Thomas was with me—we had some bottled porter in the booth—I cannot say how much we had—it did me no harm—I left the booth sober—I cannot tell what we paid—I am certain I paid 1 s., but whether I paid more, I cannot say—it was a joint payment, a club—I will swear my share did not come to 2 s.—I had my brother and sisters with me, and believe I paid more than most of them—I am not positive I did not pay 10 s., or 50 s.—I might have paid 20 s.—I do not believe I did—I am quite sure I did not pay 10 s.—nor 5 s., but you speak to me in such a curious sort of way—my brother Thomas pulled the prisoner's wife away with his hands—he did not pull her petticoats up, to my knowledge—I will not swear about it—I saw him pull her away —I saw him catch hold of her shoulders, and pull her away—I will not swear he did not pull her petticoats up for the purpose of exposing her—I was looking on, and saw him pull her away quite decently—I do not know whether she was knocked down or not—I here were other people round—I saw my brother pull her away—whether she fell down, I cannot say—I did not see her petticoats pulled up—I will not swear they were not—there were people standing before me so as to prevent my seeing—I told my brother I was done for—I do not recollect saying "Pitch into him"—I will not swear I did not say so—I was acquainted with Cults—he was not one of the party drinking with us—I do not know how the quarrel arose—my brother had been in my company up to the quarrel, and did not know how it arose.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Were you perfectly sober when this took place? A. Yes.—I did not interfere with the prisoner's wife at all—I did not see her knocked down, or her clothes disturbed—it was rather a dark evening, and being in the forest, made it still darker.
THOMAS AXON . I was returning with my brother from Fairlop-fair—we were quite sober—I have known Cutts some time—I saw him in our way home, fighting with the prisoner—Cutts called out to me, "Halloo, Tom, is that you?"—I said, "Yes, Jem, is that you?"—they went on fighting, and both fell—I he prisoner's wife kicked Cutts as he laid on the ground, and I then interfered—I went to take the prisoner off from Cutts, and his wife struck me—I shoved her with my arm, and she fell— It might be with my shoving her back with my arm, I cannot say—she got up and struck me again—I did not ill-use her in any way—I saw the prisoner make two hits at my brother—I did not see any thing in his hand at the time—I laid hold of him, and so did my brother—my brother said something to me—I let go of the prisoner, and went to him—I observed that he had been stabbed in the thigh—his trowsers had a hole in
them, and blood likewise—I went for a doctor, who came and attended him—he was confined to his room eight or nine days—I saw the wound.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You were all very sober that night? A. Yes—I am a labourer, and work for my father at jobbing, or any thing—he is a pork-butcher, and keeps a shop at Romford—I serve in the shop but very little—I work in the field sometimes, and sometimes in the shop—I do not get my living at fairs and races—we had only been in one booth that night—I he Crown and Anchor—I know Cutts from living close by—he is a gardener—I did not hear Cutts say, "I want to fight, but the b—r won't let me"—my brother and I had had a bottle of porter together—my sisters were with us—we had three bottles between us all—I here were six or seven of us—my brother, my two sisters, Carter, a horse-dealer, and myself—I cannot tell who paid for the drink—I did not—I believe Carter did—he stood treat—we found the fight going on as we were returning from the fair—I went up to them as, I saw Cutts lying down, and the prisoner holding him by the throat—I did not say, "Pitch into him, the b—," nor did I hear it said—my brother did not say so—he never interfered at all—I did not hear Cutts say, "I want to fight, but the b—won't let me"—he was standing up when I went by, and he called to me—he was afraid of getting killed, I pose—I was taking a walk at the fair—I had no pea and thimble—the prisoner's wife was standing alongside when Cutts called out to me—I went up to take the prisoner off Cutts—I did not pull his wife's petticoats over her head—I did not pull her petticoats up at all, nor meddle with her, only by shoving her away, as she was kicking Cutts—I did not take hold of her by her shoulders—that I swear—as I went to take the prisoner away she hit me, and I pushed her away—my arm came about her shoulder—I did not strike her with my fist—she fell—I did not take her by the shoulders before she fell—her petticoats did not go up at all.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Did you do anything indecent to her clothes or any thing? A. I did not—I did not go up to interfere till Cutts was on the ground.
JAMES BAILEY . I work as a gardener, at Romford. I was present and saw the best part of the fight between Cutts and the prisoner, and staid till it was over—after it was over I saw the prisoner go and strike Joseph Axon in the side two or three times, with a knife I pose, but I did not know it was a knife when he struck him—my brother John was there—I went up to the prisoner and asked if he had got a knife in his hand—he said, "Yes, and I mean to use it too"—I hat was after he had used it to the prosecutor—he had it in his hand—I did not see him put it any where—I saw the blade of a knife in his hand—that was about half a minute after I saw him strike Axon in the side—I afterwards saw Axon bleeding.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How far does Axon live from Fairlop-fair? A. Between three and four miles—he walked home—I had not been along with Axon in the fair.
COURT. Q. Did you notice when the prisoner's wife was thrown down? A. No, I did not—I saw her pulled away, and it was almost immediately after that I saw the prisoner strike Axon.
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Strongly recommended to mercy on account of the provocation. — Confined Six Months.

2274. THOMAS WRIGHT was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, 3 tame fowls, price 4 s. 6 d. the goods of James Childs.
JAMES CHILDS . I live at Woodford. I lost three fowls—I did not miss them till the policeman took the prisoner with them—they had been kept in a pig-sty or shed, but the door was fast—Ihe prisoner goes about horse-keeping.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What are you? A. I am a foreman on the trust road. I saw these fowls safe on the 18th—they were gone on the 19th, and I saw them again on the 20th.
THOMAS BARTLETT ( police-constable K 286.) On the morning of the 20th of August, at four o'clock, I met the prisoner at Stratford—I took him—I found on him this pair of scissors, these eight snares, and the three fowls—the prosecutor identified the fowls—I these snares are to catch hares or rabbits.
GUILTY .* Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2275]
2275. ROBERT ROUNDING was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, 1 shovel, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of William Blunt.
WILLIAM BLUNT . I lost my shovel on the 15th of May, about eight o'clock in the evening, out of Woodford-forest—I know the prisoner, but he was not working there.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When had you the shovel safe? A. On the 15th of May—I saw it again on the 18th—I was working as a labourer there—we are not in the habit of changing or bartering our shovels—I saw it on the 18th at Mr. Pearson's lodge, about three quarters of a mile from where I was at work.
JAMES FOSTER . I bought this shovel of the prisoner on the 15th of May.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not think it very unusual for a man to sell you a shovel? A. There was nothing suspicious in it—I bought it in the high road—I paid 1 s. 6 d. for it.
BENJAMIN HURRELL . About eight o'clock in the evening, on the 15th of May, I saw the prisoner take the shovel and put it in a bush—I was with him—I wished him to put it back again.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he put it back? A. No—he told me he knew the party, and he would have a lark—he left it there.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.

BYHAM LAWS . I live at Stratford. The prisoner was in my service occasionally—I misssed these two articles—I he prisoner had an opportunity of gettig them.
MARIA PERKS . I am the prosecutor's housekeeper. I know these articles are his—I he prisoner came to the house soccasionally.
GEORGE SMITH ( police-constable K 38.) I produce these articles, which I found in a box, which the prisoner said was hers, except this shift.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the two shifts myself, the silk to make myself a bonnet, and I bought the apron—I had no handkerchief in my box—my sister Kit has done it out of spite—she broke my box and pawned my gowns, and then she brought the policeman to search my box.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Nine Months.
