2279. THOMAS GRANT was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of August, at Lewisham, 12 forks, value 12 l., 29 spoons, value 16 l. 10 s., 2 ladles, value 3 l.; and 4 knife-rests, value 10 s.; the goods of William Abbott, in his dwelling-house.
THOMAS BODDY . I am servant to William Abbott, of Meadow-croft, Sydenham, in the parish of Lewisham. On the 11th of August, I left the dining-room—I he table was prepared for dinner, and the plate-basket was by the side of the sideboard, with spoons and forks in it, and some on the table—I left the window a little up—it looks into the road—I left the room at three o'clock, and went out—I afterwards, in consequence of information, went into the room, and missed the articles stated—I gave
information to the police, and have since seen the articles—they are worth above 5 l.—I these produced are them.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What is your master's name? A. William—he has no other name—I have been two years with him—his crest is on the articles.
REV. EDWARD KNIGHT MADDOCK . I was on a visit at Meadow-croft. On the afternoon of Wednesday, between five and six o'clock, the 11th of August, I met the prisoner near Mr. Abbott's—I here was a boy near him but not immediately by him—I noticed that the prisoner dropped two spoons, and took them up again—in consequence of what I observed, I went in the same direction as he went, and afterwards met him with the same lad—I asked the prisoner if I had not seen him just now at Mea-dow-croft—he said, "No," and I suffered him to go on then—and after going a few paces, he started off across the field, making a motion to the boy to run too—I hey ran in parallel lines, but one diverging from the other—I was alone—I met a painter, who joined me, and we saw the prisoner in a hedge—I had not lost sight of him till I found him in the hedge—he was crouching down—after I came up, I was on one side of the hedge and the painter on the other, and he started up from the hedge and ran in a different direction, and I saw the painter scuffling with him—I afterwards pointed out the spot where he had been crouching down, to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he apprehended there? A. No, he got away from there—I first saw him between five and six o'clock, I think—he was about thirty yards from the prosecutor's house then, and I was going towards it—I did not turn round to follow him till I had been into the house where I was visiting—I met him again in about half an hour going up towards the Dartmouth Arms public-house, not in a direction for the prosecutor's house—I met him at first coming in a direction from the prosecutor's house—after I had made inquiry, I followed him in the same tract, and met him coming back—I said to him, "Did I see you?"but I had no doubt on the subject—I am quite certain he is the person who dropped the spoons—I wished for positive proof of his guilt—I did not know then that any plate was lost—I might pose he was going to match I plate for his master, who might be a jeweller.
JOHN BEGLE . I am a painter and glazier, and live at Sydenhara. On the 14th of August, I saw the prisoner running in a field, and Mr. Mad-dock running after him—I came up to him, he was then lying in a hedge, and rushed out upon me—he took something out of his pocket with his left-hand, which looked like a knife—I laid hold of him, but he got from me—he had his coat closed with his hands, and when he came out of to hedge he let go of his coat—I here appeared to be something in his coat—I did not see him taken—I know the spot where he was crouched down.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe the other party has not been taken? A. Not that I know—I he prisoner squared at me, when I came up, with one hand, and his other hand was in his left-hand trowsers pocket.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable R 122.) I produce some gravy spoons, dessert spoons, and forks, which I found in the hedge, within half a yard of where the Rev. Mr. Maddock pointed out, in a lane at Syden-ham, wrapped up in a handkerchief.
Cross-examined. Q. How far was it from where Mr. Maddock pointed
out? A. Close to it—Mr. Maddock showed me the place where he was found in the hedge—I did not show the spot to the last witness.
THOMAS CAMPBELL ( police-constable R 140.) I produce four dessert spoons, a sauce ladle, and tea-spoon, which I found in the lane, through the hedge, in the same spot—I they had dropped through out of the handkerchief, as my brother-constable took it up.
RICHARD HOBBS ( police-constable R 138.) I found the prisoner crouched down in a ditch in the corner of Stansbeard-lane—I collared him and pulled him out—he said he was sorry he had not been over the other side of the hedge, and then he should have been two or three miles off—I said, "It is a pity, then, but what you had been on the other side"—he said he was very sorry he had not, and what a parcel of d—fools we most all he to stand there waiting for him—I and my brother-constable took him to the station.
JURY. Q. Do you know where the plate was found,? A. Yes, I took him about a quarter of a mile from there—I he hedge runs all along there—the plate was found in one field, and he in the adjoining field.
Q. How could he get from one to the other? A. There is a cross hedge and a bean field on the other side—he must have gone over the hedge—I took him between six and seven o'clock.
ANN BURNETT . I live at Brockley Farm. On the 11th of August, I saw the prisoner in Eliza-place, two miles' from Sydenham, with a red parcel—I soon afterwards saw some persons running after him—he ran across the field, but I did not see him put the parcel into the hedge—I was visiting at Eliza-place at the time—I he parcel was in a handkerchief with red spots, such as the one produced.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you known him before? A. No, I am quite certain of him—he was standing about thirty-yards from me when I first saw him—I have not the least doubt of him.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18410823-2280]
2280. LAZARUS ISAACS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of August, 1 pair of trowsers, value 20 s., the goods of John Nicholas Harris; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN RIXON . I am a stationer, and live in Richard-street, Woolwich, right opposite Mr. Harris. On the 9th of August, I saw the prisoner at his shop-door—he went in, went to the other end of the shop, took the trowsers off a peg, rolled them up, put them into a basket which he had, and was returning with them, when I secured him—he was trying to push the trowsers out of the basket when I caught hold of him—I did not let Dim get out of the shop.
Prisoner. I went in to ask the lady if she wanted any lemons; the corner of the trowsers hung to my basket, which was on my shoulder, and fell down—I was going to pick them up, when the gentleman came over and gave me in charge. Witness. They did not fall down—he put them into his basket, and they were half out when I got hold of him—he was professing to sell lemons—he had the basket, under his arm hanging by a strap round his neck.
JOHN NICHOLAS HARRIS . I am a tailor, and live in Richard-street, Woolwich. These are my trowsers, they are worth 1 l., and are nearly new.
JOHN WATERMAN ( police-constable R 44.) The prisoner told me they hung to the corner of his basket.
WILLIAM PARRAMORE . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at his trial, and know him to be the person described in that certificate, though he was then tried by the name of Eleazer Levy—( read)—I have not seen him since then.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2281]
2281. STEWART M'KOWN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of July, 1 washing-board, value 1 s., the goods of George Cousins.
GEORGE COUSINS . I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery. Some few weeks ago I lost a washing-board and tea-kettle from a washhouse at the back of Artillery-place—I afterwards saw the washing-board at Saville's shop, on the 2nd of July—I hat now produced is it—I his case was not taken up by the regiment, on account of the prisoner's character.
JOHN SAVILLE . I am a broker, in Wellington-street, Woolwich, bought this washing-board of the prisoner for 6 d.
JOHN THOROGOOD ( police-constable R 189.) I took the prisoner into custody—I asked him whether he knew any thing of the property I produced—he said he knew nothing of it—I asked if he would deny it if I brought a witness who bought the property of him—he said, if that was the case, it was all well and good.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor and the witness are related together, and it is a combined thing between them against me; he does not live six doors from me; it is not likely I should steal it, and sell it to a man that knew me.
JOHN SAVILLE re-examined. I never knew the prisoner before—I am no acquaintance of Cousins.
GEORGE COUSINS re-examined. He is a gunner in the regiment, the same as myself—he has a good-conduct stripe on his arm—I here has been no quarrel between us—he has been convicted twice in the regiment for theft—a man is entitled to a stripe if he is two years out of trouble—it is a long time since he was in trouble—I never knew him before this occurrence—he belongs to the 9th battalion, and I to the first—the property was locked up in the washhouse—I he prisoner rented a room in the same house for a very short time—there is no one here from the regiment to give him a character—the officers are aware he is on trial—I had used this washing-board a week before—it was not thrown aside as if it was lumber—the prisoner's wife asked if we had got such a thing to lend her, and when we came to look for it it was gone—I here has been no quarrel between his wife and mine—I he board was exposed for sale at Saville's which is not far from where I live—I know it by marks where I pulled the head off, and nailed it on again myself—I bought it in Woolwich.
GUILTY. Aged 23.— Strongly recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Confined Ten Days.
Before Mr. Justice Coleridge.
[trialAccount t18410823-2282]
2282. EDWARD STUDD was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Frances Studd.
ELIZABETH DALTON . I know the prisoner, and knew his wife, France Studd—Ihey lived in Old King-street. On the 25th of June, about two
o'clock, I saw them talking together—they appeared to be quarrelling—I was twenty or thirty yards from them—I saw the prisoner strike her with his shut fist on the left side of the head—she was standing on the pavement—she staggered a little, and then she fell on her side into the road—the prisoner walked away, leaving her there—she got up, and I and Mrs. Ires went up to her—I here was a little scratch just over her right temple, where she fell, and there might be one drip of blood—she complained of her head, where she fell—I he prisoner walked as far as the Rodney public-house—she got up, and went after him, and they were talking together again—I did not follow her, but stood looking after them—I saw her next on the Thursday following, seven days after, in Old King-street—she then appeared to be very ill, and was going to the doctor's—I cannot say whether the prisoner was sober or not on the 25th of June—I had never seen him before—I saw him knock her down, and know him to be the roan—his lips were frothed—I did not take much notice of him—I looked at his wife, as I thought she was hurt—he only struck her once—Ihey were both standing still when I came towards them—I did not see him walk up to her—I was examined before the Magistrate—what I said was read over to me, and I signed it, and also what I said before the Coroner.
BRIDGET IVES . I am the wife of Isaac Ives, and the sister of Dalton. I know the prisoner, and have seen his wife twice. On the 25th of June I was with my sister, coming down King-street, and just before we got opposite the King's Arms public-house I saw the prisoner and his wife standing on the pavement—I saw him hit her on the left side of the head—she fell on her right side into the road—I had not heard them talking together before, but they seemed to be talking—she got up, and went to the other side of the road, and he walked in the middle—when they got about twenty yards, she went to him—he had only hit her once, but he made an offer to hit her then—she put her hand into her pocket, and he went into the Cheroot public-house—we went to where he knocked her down, and she showed us the mark where he had hit her in the head, and the scratch where she fell—I here is a pavement there—I saw her next Thursday coining from the doctor's—she looked very bad, and had her head bound up in a white cloth—I saw nothing more of the prisoner—I cannot say whether he was sober or drunk.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You will not swear he was sober? A. No.
JURY. Q. Was his wife sober? A. Yes—I am sure of that.
WILLIAM ATKINS . I am a surgeon. On the 26th of June the deceased came to me—I had known her for a few months, but had not attended her before—she complained of violent pains in her head—I examined it, but could perceive no external cause for it—she did not point to any part of her head—I gave her medicine, treating it as a common pain in the head, which frequently occurs—she came again on the 27th, and said she thought herself rather better—she came again on the 28th, and stated she was much the same—I saw her again on Wednesday the 30th, when I was requested to visit her—I found her considerably worse, and in bed—I ordered mustard plasters to various parts of the body, treating it as a case of inflammation, and ordered leeches to the temple on account of the inflammation—there were no external marks of inflammation—she appeared in violent pain all over the head—she was not exactly sensible—she was not comatose—there was a wandering state of mind—I saw her again the first
thing oh Thursday morning, the 1st of July—she was then much worse—she died on Saturday evening, the 3rd—I opened her head on Monday and found very extensive inflammation—I he sinuses of the brain filled with serous fluid, and the whole of the brain having marks of extensive inflammation—I found no external bruise, except a little scar on the left temple, which might have happened months before, for it was perfectly healed—my opinion is, from the history of the case during the time I treated it, that her death was caused from a blow received—I attribute her death to inflammation of the brain—I did not find any local injury within the skull—she was in a very emaciated state.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Might not the inflammation you have stated be as well occasioned by hitting against the curbstone, if she had fallen? A. Such a thing might he—we have cases of pains of the head daily—I do not remember a case where I could not trace the cause of inflammation—intemperance would cause inflammation of the brain—I should not naturally expect to find some external mark from the description given by the witnesses—inflammation might arise without external marks—if she fell on the curb-stone I should certainly expect to find a mark, or if she had been struck against any hard substance—I knew nothing of the woman's habits.
JURY. Q. When she first came to you did not she draw your attention to marks on her head? A. None whatever—she merely said she had received a violent blow on the left side of her head on the previous evening—if a scar had been made so as to draw blood I should have seen it, or if there was a scar where she fell—I examined her very minutely indeed.
JOHN THOMAS TAYLOR . I am a parish constable. I apprehended the prisoner at the Jury-room.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe when you spoke to him, he said he hid struck his wife, and was very sorry for it? A. Yes, and seemed very penitent.
MR. PHILLIPS called
ESTHER FRANCIS . I am the wife of a Greenwich pensioner. I know the prisoner, and knew his wife—we lived in the same house—I think if the man had had a wife accordingly, he would have been as good a husband it ever was—she kept very bad hours—I never saw him hit her—she used to come in at all times in the night—she used to come up to our room, and greatly to annoy us—at times she was perfectly sober, and I have seen her quite tipsy, not often—I saw her once up at the turnpike after twelve o'clock, quite tipsy—her late hours were frequent—I they did not live in our house at the time this happened, and I know nothing of it.
COURT. Q. How long before the 25th of June bad you seen her? A. She came over to my house, and said her head was very bad—she was subject to a very bad head—she always complained of her head ever since I knew her, which was ten months.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2287. CHARLES BELL was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of August, 1 ring, value 12 s.; 1 breast-pin, value 5 s.; 1 ink-stand, value 1 s.; and 1 printed book, value 2 s.; the goods of Jemima Daniels.
JEMIMA DANIELS . I am single, and reside in Prospect-cottages, Shooter's-hill. On the morning of the 8th of August, I found my bureau had been broken open—it stood in my wash-house, and these articles were missing—I he ink-stand and book produced are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What day did you miss them? A. On Sunday, the 8th of August, they were taken—I did not discover it till Monday morning.
MARIAN ESTHER DANIELS . I am single, and live with my sister—the bureau was secure on the Sunday.
SUSANNAH MARTHA PICKETT . I am servant to Miss Daniels—I he prisoner used to work in the house—on this Sunday, I saw him putting the ink-stand into his pocket—he was then at the bureau—he had the lid of the bureau in his hand, and a chisel was on the top of the bureau—I said, "Charley, what are you doing?"—he held up his finger, and said, "Hush "—I said, "What are you doing?"—he said, "I shall have this," and put the ink-stand into his pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. What age are you? A. Twelve—I he prisoner is servant to Mrs. Phillips, who lives in the next house—she was repairing the lower part of her house, and the prisoner came into our wash-house to clean his shoes—I did not say any thing about this, as I was afraid the prisoner would beat me—he used very often to beat me—he once hit me on the arm with a hammer—he did not threaten to beat me for this—I told my mistress on Monday.
WILLLIAM GEORGE OSBORN ( police-sergeant R 13.) I found a chisel on
the top of the bureau, and the marks on the bureau appeared to have been made by it.
JOHN WALKER ( police-constable R 182.) When the prisoner had been taken, I heard him crying at the window of the station—I asked him what it was about—he said two old ladies who lived at Prospect-cottages, Shooter's-hill, had lost a ring and a pin—he said he knew nothing about them, but the ink-stand was by the manger—I went there, and found it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Four Days, and Whipped.

MARY ANN COTTON . 2289. JOSEPH KEATS was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of August, 1 peck of a certain mixture of oats and beans, value 1 s., and 2 bushels of chaff, value 1 s., the goods of William Pershouse.
JOHN WALKER . I am a policeman. On the 21st of August, at half-past eleven o'clock at night, I was in the Trafalgar-road, at Greenwich—I saw the prisoner go into a stable. belonging to Mr. Pershouse—he had nothing with him then—he was in about a quarter of an hour—he then came out with a sack on his back—he shut the door, and was going away—he got two or three yards off with the sack, when I took him by the shoulder and said I should take him to Mr. Pershouse—he said, "For God's sake don't take me, for the sake of my character; I will give you all the money I have got, which is 5 l."—when I seized him, he dropped the sack.
Prisoner. I never said such a thing—I took the sack out of my cart, which I had there.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was there a cart? A. I did not see it—I was within three yards of the stable door, standing behind some pales, which concealed me, so that I could see the prisoner, and he could not see me—I had been there three quarters of an hour.
COURT. Q. What did the sack contain? A. Beans, oats, and chaff, and some bits of brown paper were amongst it, that the prosecutor might identify it.
ROBERT TAYLOR ( police-sergeant R 18.) I was with the other officer—what he has stated is correct.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see any cart? A. No, I was standing behind a gate, which has some open palings—I was on one side of the gate, and Taylor on the other.
WILLIAM PERSHOUSE . This was my stable. I had been robbed, and told the officer to watch—I mixed this oats, beans, and chaff, so as to know it, about an hour and a half before it was taken—I had cut some brown paper and put among it—I know it to be the same—the prisoner was brought to me by the two officers—he said he brought the corn from the dealers—I said, "It is not worth while to tell stories, you will see when you get to the station that there is a mark in it"—he then said, "It was pot handy for me."
(James Kite, a haberdasher, of Greenwich, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.

2294. JOHN FERNAN was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE AUGUSTUS BEAURAIN . I am clerk to Mr. George Franks, a
surgeon, in Blackfriars-road—The prisoner was in his service, On the 7th of August, I gave him a sovereign to fetch'oil—I am in the habit of paying for the oil used in the bouse—we always have two gallons at a time—the prisoner returned and gave me a shilling—The price we usually pay is 9 s. 6 d. a gallon—he was to go to Zetterquist's, in Blackfriars-road, first—he was not told to go there then, but I have told him before, and it is where we dealt.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Do you mean there is any particular connexion between Mr. Zetterquist and the prosecutor, which made it necessary to get the oil there? A. No, but at the beginning of winter we could not get good oil, and on that occasion I told him to go to Zetterquist's—I do not mean to say he was obliged to go there always—he was master's groom and porter—he was not told to buy two gallons—I did not examine how much oil there was in the house when he went—whether there might be another gallon in the house which he bought elsewhere, I cannot tell—it was Mr. Franks's sovereign I gave him—I took it out of the cash-box.
MR. ESPINASSE. Q. Did you know whether your master was in the habit of dealing with Zetterquist? A. Yes, for various things—The prisoner was told to go there, and we thought he was in the habit of going there.
ELISHA KNIGHT . I am an oilman, and live in Charlotte-terrace, New Cut. On the morning of the 7th of August, the prisoner came to my shop, and bought one gallon of oil—he gave me a sovereign—I gave him 11 s. change—I think it was a five-shilling piece, two half-crowns, and a shilling—he returned to the shop a little after one o'clock, and said his master and a friend had been inquiring about the oil, and if they came he hoped I should say it was two gallons he bad—he said he had purchased one gallon a short time before, which he paid for out of his own pocket—he said if I did not say so, he should get the cook and housemaid, and all of them into a scrape, that they would get the sack —he staid some time begging hard of me to say it was two gallons—I told him it was a system I did not approve of, and if he had purchased a gallon before, it would be quite satisfactory to Mr. Franks to say so—I urged him to tell the truth, saying it would be a gross falsehood on my part to say it was two when it I was only one—Mr. Franks and Mr. Harvey then came in.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known the prisoner? A. About a month—he had not purchased another gallon of oil at my shop, that I am aware of—I have only served him once—he was ten minutes with me desiring me to say he had had two gallons—his master then came in—he spoke to him, and then went away—I live very nearly the same distance from the prosecutor's as Mr. Zetterquist does—it is not two minutes difference—when the prisoner came the second time, he bought 1 d. worth of rotten-stone to polish harness.
GEORGE FRANKS . I am a surgeon. I was at home on the 7th of August, when the prisoner came in and put 1 s. down—he said, "That is the change from the oil"—he did not say what quantity—I asked him where he got the oil—he said from Mr. Knight, in the Cut (I had been to Irongalf Wharf, and meant to go again) I told him to go below and forward his work and to get his dinner, and at three o'clock to put the horse to, and I would go with him to Irongate Wharf—shortly after I went with Mr. Harvey, a friend of mine, and an acquaintance of Mr. Knight's, to Knight's shop—Mr. Harvey
went through the shop into the parlour, and I saw the prisoner come out from the parlour in about five minutes—I said, "What do you do here?"—he said he came to speak to Mr. Knight about the oil, as he thought I seemed to suspect all was not right—I said he had no business to do that, I did not complain about the oil—I had not expressed any suspicion up to that time—I told him to go home—he then said he came to boy some harness polish, which he had in his hand—after saying a few words to Mr. Knight, I got a policeman and gave him into custody—The prisoner asked why I did so—I said it was on a charge of felony—he said, "I know, sir, that I have done wrong respecting the oil, I assure yon that I bought a gallon about ten days back, that I ought then to have mentioned it to Mr. George; I know that it is very wrong that I did not"—I said, "Well, John, but you have charged 9 s. 6 d. a gallon, and only paid Mr. Knight 9 s."—he replied he certainly did pay Mr. Knight 9 s. 6 d.—he then alluded to the box at the Irongate Wharf—I said, "Well, you will put on your coat and go with the police," and I gave him in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had he been in your service? A. Six or seven months—in that time I pose he bought six or eight gallons of oil for me—I did not see how much oil I had in my possession at this time—I cannot tell whether he had bought any within ten days or not—he gave me that account immediately on my speaking to him—according to the consumption of oil in my house, it is not likely it should have been bought and used—I should say there ought not to have been any wanted—The prisoner said, "If you measure the oil in the house, yon will find there was the gallon I have just now bought, and about half a gallon, which was in the can before I went for it"—I did not measure—I had no particular reason for not doing so.
Q. Supposing you had measured, and found the gallon newly brought, and the half-gallon also remaining, would that have satisfied you that what he said was correct? A. It would not certainly, because there would have been no reason for his getting the oil if there was some in the house—half a gallon will last us for some time—he never paid for oil out of his own pocket—I should say the two gallons we had before ought to have lasted—I do not, of my own knowledge, know when those two gallons were purchased, only from the books.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18410823-2295]
2295. JOHN FERNAN was again indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering a forged and altered receipt for the sum of 1 s. 6 d., with intent to defraud George Franks.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE AUGUSTUS BEAURAIN . I am clerk to Mr. Franks, the prisoner was in his service. On Friday, the 6th of August, I gave him a parcel to take to the Irongate-wharf, and book it for Dublin—here is the book he took with him—I did not give him any money at the time I gave him the parcel—he afterwards produced this receipt to me—I told him it had been altered, that the 6 d. appeared to be added—he said it was just as he received it, that they took it back again from him, and he did not know whether they had altered it then—I paid him 18 d., the sum mentioned in the receipt—I afterwards made a communication to Mr. Franks—it was Mr. Franks's money I paid him.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPIIUS. Q. Whose writing is that book? A. This direction of the parcel was written by me before he went.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was all that is written in the last line written except the sum received and the initials of the person receiving it? A. All of it.
CHARLES RANFORD . I am clerk at the Irongate-wharf. I remember a person bringing a parcel to go by the Dublin steam-packet on Friday, the 6th of August—I received 1 s. for it—this is the book the person I bringing the parcel presented to me—the 1 s. written in this book is Kingston's, our foreman's, writing—it has his initial at the end—I received the money, and gave him the receipt now produced—I he "6 d." has been added to it since—it was a receipt for 1 s.—no other charge was made at the wharf.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he take the book away with him after your foreman wrote in it? A. Yes—I cannot swear to the prisoner, but it was this book which the person brought.
COURT. Q. Did you see the book yourself? A. Yes, I made the charge from the book.
JOSEPH KENT . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in charge on Saturday, the 7th of August.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not he say 18 d. had been paid when you took him? A. He did say so.
GUILTY .— Confined Two Years, to commence from the expiration of the former Sentence.
Before Mr. Recorder.

2296. MARY ANN RYAN was indicted for feloniously receiving, of a certain evil-disposed person, on the 30th of June, 4 bonnets, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of George Hunt, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MARY HUNT . I am the wife of George Hunt, of Well-street, Southampton-street, Camberwell; I keep a straw-bonnet shop. On the 30th of June, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon, three young women came into my shop and looked at some Tuscan bonnets, but bought nothing—they were about to leave, when a customer came in—one of them then selected a bonnet at 10 s. 6 d., and placed it on the counter for me to wrap up; and in returning to place the other bonnets this was among into a box which was on the ground, she stooped down, as if she was putting them into the box, and hastily quitted the shop—I directly went to the box and missed four bonnets—I he young women were convicted at Honemonger-lane on my charge—I here was another charge against them also—I have the four bonnets here—I they are all mine, and what I lost on that occasion, three Tuscan and one Rutland.
JAMES TIDMARSH . I am apoliceman. On the 30th of June I saw the prisoner, in the Walworth-road, about six o'clock in the evening—I follwed her a considerable distance, nearly three-quarters of a mile, towards the Borough, when I saw another constable—I stopped her, and asked what she had got in her basket—she said she had her own property—I open it, and found seven bonnets there—I hese are four of them—she said she bought them in the lane, meaning Rosemary-lane—I stopped her about a mile from the prosccutrix's shop, within an hour of the robbery.
Witnesses for the Defence.
MARY M'CARTHY . I live in Peter-street, Soho. I have known the prisoner for the last ten years—she is a dealer in clothes—she has borne a very honest character—I have dealt in the fair for the last ten years—it is in Cutler-street, Petticoat-lane—it goes by the name of the Lane—all sorts of men and women's apparel is sold there, old bonnets, old hats, and every thing—it is very much frequented by Jews.
WILLIAM HENNESSEY . I am an engineer and smith. I have known the prisoner twenty years—I know she frequents the fair in Petticoat-lane and Cutler-street—sometimes there are so many people there you cannot get your foot through it; sometimes the policemen cannot get a passage through—she has borne a most undeniable character.
COURT. Q. Do you deal at the fair? A. No—I cannot say whether things are cheap there—they sell all kinds of apparel—I have seen Mrs. Kilday, the prisoner, there—I mean Ryan, Kilday is her former husband's name.
ELLEN MYERS . I am a widow, and live in Paradise-row. I have known the prisoner fifteen years to be a general-dealer in Petticoat-lane—I never heard of any charge against her—she has borne quite an honest character—I have got my living there all that time, with the exception of the Jews' holidays.
COURT. Q. What are the sort of dealings there? are goods sold at any price? A. There is everything, from 1 d. to 10 s. or 5 s.—we do not inquire where they come from, we have no business to do so; it is an open fair—what we buy in the fair we sell again before we leave—I put it on my arm, and sell it back again if I can get 1 s. or 6 d.—I do not suppose any body would bring things dishonestly come by to an open place like that.
MARY HUNT re-examined. The prisoner is not one of the three who took the bonnets—I he four bonnets together are worth about 2 l.—the three women left my shop as near five o'clock as possible.
JAMES TIDMARSH , re-examined. I first saw the prisoner about six o'clock, about half or three-quarters of a mile from the prosecutrix's house, and about four miles and a half from Petticoat-lane—I stopped her in Newington-causeway.
GUILTY . † Aged 59.— Confined Eighteen Months.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

2297. MARY PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of August, at Bermondsey, 7 sovereigns, the monies of Peter Barralls, in his dwelling-house.
MARY BARRALLS . I am the wife of Peter Barralls, and live in Medley's place, in the parish of Bermondsey—it is his dwelling-house. On the 24th of August the prisoner came to assist me in washing—I had some sovereigns wrapped up in a sock which I kept in a little press-bedstead—I saw them safe in the morning—she was gone, and I missed the sovereigns and the sock—I went and spoke to her about it in the morning—she told me she had taken them for safety—she said, "Yes, my dear, I have got them, and will return them to you again"—she put her hand into her pocket, took out a paper bag, and took out six sovereigns—I asked her what had become of the seventh—she told me she had changed it, and bought these things with it.
Prisoner's Defence." She asked me if I would go up stairs and make
the press-bed for a woman who was going to sleep in the house, as she was going to make away with herself; I said, "How can you think of doing so?" I made the bed, and this bag and money dropped out; I said to the little girl, "Take this purse down to your mother," and she took it down to her mother; I picked the sock up on the landing, and was going to throw it in the dust-hole.
MARY BARRALLS re-examined. I had hid it in the press-bedstead for some time before that—there is no pretence for saying she came to take care of me.
GUILTY .— Confined Six Months.
