2298. JOSEPH THOMPSON was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Daniel Birth, on the 14th of August, at Streatham, and stealing therein, 11 spoons, value 6 l.; 1 ladle, value 10 s.; and 9 forks, value 1 l. 10 s.; his goods.
ELIZABETH MERRITT . On the 14th of August I was in the service of Mr. Daniel Birth, of Upper Tooting—in the course of Saturday afternoon, the 14th of August, I was in the hall—there is a coach-house adjoining the house, under the same roof, and a communication from the coach-house into the house by a side door—I observed that the side door was shut half an hour before, but the coach-house door was open—I beard a noise of the gingling of plate—the plate was kept in a pantry—my master does not keep a footman—the housemaid takes care of the plate—I had seen it in the pantry ten minutes before I heard the tinkling—on hearing it I ran down stairs, and saw two men running out at the coach-house door—I ran down stairs, and was close on them—I saw them through the side door—I hey had plate in their hands—the prisoner was one of them—had never seen him before, but I am quite certain he is the person—I ran after them, and called out "Thief"—they ran up a lane opposite the dwelling-house., and I after them, but lost sight of them—about four or five minutes after, I saw the prisoner brought back by Francis—he had nothing on him then—I went into the pantry, and missed the plate stated in the indictment—these four table-spoons, three dessert forks, and three German silver forks now produced are Mr. Bin's property.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you speak to Francis about it before the prisoner was brought back by him? A. I told him the house had been robbed—I was not more than two yards from the persons I saw running away from the coach-house door at first, but when they jumped over the fence I lost sight of them—I saw the prisoner's face as he was opening the latch-gate to leave the premises—I saw his face quite plain—I am not swearing to him by his dress at all—he was dressed the same as be is now—I am positive of that, and am quite sure he is the same person—I have always been so—I saw him searched, but nothing was found on him.
THOMAS FRANCIS . I am a jobbing gardener, and work for Mr. Birth sometimes. On the 14th of August I heard Merritt shout out "Thief—she came up the lane, and gave an alarm that the house had been robbed of some plate—I and Benfield followed the prisoner into the garden of a woman named Cockett—Benfield said he thought he saw something underneath the beans—I went into the beans, and there found the prisoner secreted—I do not know whether he was lying or crouching down—the beans were seven or eight feet high—Benfield searched the place, and found
the plate—I caught hold of the prisoner, and Benfield assisted me, and went for the police—the prisoner said, "What do you want of me?"—I said, "I want that plate you have brought from over the way from Mr. Birt's house"—he said, "What plate? I have no plate"—and when the policeman searched him he had nothing on him—it was about 130 or 140 yards from Mr. Birt's house that I found him, and about five minutes after Merritt spoke to me.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not see any other person? A. No, only one—I saw Benfield find the plate, and he gave it into my hands immediately—he found it on the ground, exactly on the spot where the prisoner was, close to him—the prisoner's clothes were not unbuttoned, that I could perceive—I did not ask him what he was doing there, and do not recollect that Benfield did—I heard nothing pass about his being there to ease himself.
COURT. Q. Where were you when Merritt spoke to you? A. At borne, about 150 yards from Mr. Benfield's—she came by where the prisoner turned, in order to come to my house—she came beyond him, twenty or thirty yards beyond the turning.
WILLIAM BENFIELD . On the 14th of August I heard Merritt give an alarm, as I was walking home, rather past the turning where the plate was found—I directly went to the corner of the turning, and looked round the corner, but saw no one—I went and looked over the railing of Mrs. Cockett's garden, and saw something move under the beans, on the ground—I called Francis, and be went in first—he went to the further end of the beans, and said, "Here is one of them"—he caught the prisoner by the tail of his coat, and I took him by the collar—I found the plate produced stuck in the ground, near to where he was, the bowls of the spoons down-wards—I saw the ground had been fresh moved, and turned it up, and found the plate—it was quite covered with earth.
Crass-examined. Q. Did you observe any thing about the prisoner's dress? A. No—it was not unfastened—I did not see him till he was brought out of the beans by Francis—the fence between the beans and the road is about three feet six inches high—it is only a rail across—there is another fence at the bottom of the beans, and over that another garden—I did not hear the prisoner say any thing about being there to ease himself.
GEORGE CHAPMAN ( police-constable V 131.) I received this plate from Benfield—I saw him take it out of the earth, and have had it in my possession ever since.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of Stealing only. Aged 20.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Justice Erskine.
[trialAccount t18410823-2299]
2299. CHARLES HENRY UNDEY , and WILLIAM HENRY DAKERS were indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Edward Lewis.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD PAYNE COTTON . I am a surgeon, and live in Kensington-square. On the 16th of August I was fishing in company with the de-ceased and some other friends—I had known the deceased about two years—his name was Edward Lewis—he was twenty years old—I began fishing with him about half-past ten o'clock—he had been fishing about two hours
previously—it was in the river, near Richmond, opposite to the Duke of Buccleugh's, which is on the Surrey side, above Richmond-bridge—I, the deceased, and two others, were in a punt, about midway, moored by stakes at each end across the punt—we were sitting on chairs—there were two other gentlemen, friends, in a skiff close by—the skiff was on the Surrey side of the punt—while we were there in the morning I remember two steamers passing up towards Richmond, on the Surrey side of the punt—that is the side on which the current is—where we were is comparatively shallow—when the steamers passed up to Twickenham in the morning there was a little swell—it did not interrupt our fishing—about half-past five o'clock I was sitting with my face towards Richmond-bridge—we were all in that position, and fishing in the same place—I looked round, and saw a steamboat coming from Twickenham—it was not more than thirty yards from the punt when I first saw it—I cannot say at what rate it was coming, but it appeared to be coming rapidly—the tide had turned about an hour, and was running down—I am unable to say if it was a strong tide—I observed somebody in some part of the boat, but I cannot say where, waving his hand as if to the man at the wheel—he was in an elevated position, or I should not have seen him—I have seen steamboats navigating, and a person on the paddle-box making a motion—it appeared to be done in that way—I was standing up—I could not see the person at the helm—the steamer continued to approach towards the Surrey side of the river—I cannot say whether it altered its course after the waving with the hand, for I sat down, thinking I should be thrown down, as I thought it would either strike the punt, or we should be upset by the swell—I had scarcely sat down a moment when we were struck by the steamboat—I had not even time to call out—I was about the middle of the punt—I know I was not on the Surrey side of it—the deceased was near the extreme end, on the Surrey side—there was a little boy sitting beyond, or nearest the Surrey side—I did not observe whether Mr. Lewis was standing or sitting at that moment—the end of the punt towards the Surrey side was struck by the steamboat—I cannot say what part of the steamer struck us—we were all knocked down into the punt, and the punt, particularly the end which was struck, went under water—that caused the punt to ship a deal of water—it did not right itself—that end remained slightly under water—directly I got up I looked round, and saw Mr. Lewis struggling in the water—the punt was driven from both its moorings, and carried down with the tide—I threw out two chairs to Mr. Lewis, and other efforts were made to save him but he sunk—I did not then observe the steamer—I afterwards saw her below the bridge—I think I saw her about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour after then, between us and the Surrey shore—I afterwards saw her below bridge, stopping—I rowed about for some time to endeavour to find the deceased, but did not succeed—I afterwards went with a policeman on board the steamboat—it is called the Richmond —the prisoner Undey was pointed out as the captain—I cannot say whether he was the person I had seen in an elevated position making signals—the policeman who was with me told him he was come to take him to the station, or something to that effect, I did not hear him say what for—Undey said he had not charge of the vessel at the time, and objected to go with the policeman—he pointed to Dakers, and said that he had charge of the vessel at the time—I did not hear Dakers say any thing—he made no objection to go—ultimately they
both went with the policeman to the station, where I preferred the charge—Undey appeared to me to have been drinking—he was not actually drunk, but I am confident he was in liquor—I afterwards saw the body of my friend—he had just been taken out—he had been in the water between half an hour and three-quarters of an hour, and was quite dead, bat he had been taken out once before that—we did all we could for him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I suppose you were naturally very much agitated at this misfortune? A. Yes—I had not seen Undey before, that I am aware of—I often go to fish there, but I never observed the steamers—I have always been intent on fishing, so that a steamer might approach from behind without my seeing it—a steamer makes a noise, hut there are other noises; so that it might not attract attention, if intent on fishing—the punt was moored across the river—I did not observe any other steamer there—I did not observe the Vivid —she might have been within sixty or eighty yards of us, and I not see her—I did not see her after the accident, I was so agitated and engaged in looking after the body, not after steamers—before the accident happened the punt was moored firm—I saw one steamer, but I believe that was the Richmond, which caused the accident—I did not see a second—it might have been there, but I was so agitated at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What were yon sitting in? A. An ordinary-sized punt, as long as from me to his Lordship, and about three or four feet wide—it was a flat-bottomed one, and I should think drew no water, but I do not understand it—I have fished there many times before in the same situation—the punt was moored stem and stern above the bridge, right athwart the stream—one of the poles to which the punt was moored had given way, and she turned and lay with the stream; but the fisherman came up again, and set her exactly in the same place as she was before, as one of the poles had not moved—it was done by pushing the pole in the river, and making a piece of rope fast to her—there were three chain in the punt, there were no sculls or oars in her—she was not moved by sculls, hut pushed along, in shallow water, by means of a boat-hook—she was square stem and stern—I know the place where the Vivid moors, when she comes up to receire her passengers—I have seen boats lying there—it is about 150 yards from where we were moored—I cannot say whether there are shallows about there—we had only been fishing about an hour when the accident happened—we had been fishing before, but in consequence of the tide not suiting, we were obliged to discontinue fishing, and then resumed it again at about four o'clock—I do not know whether it was about flood—we resumed it when the fisherman told us we might do so—the fisherman put us on board—I cannot say whether the punt was injured at all—I have not seen it since—if I had had the curiosity to look, I could not have done it, for I was taken out of the punt a very few minutes after the accident happened—she was filling with water—she did not go down—I cannot say whether she would not go down—I did not then observe any mark on the punt—the steamer's striking her dragged her from her moorings—there may have been some injury done to her, but I do not know whether there was or not—I have not looked—the chairs we were sitting on were only a few inches above the level of the water—I should not think the ripple of a sailing-barge would place us in very great jeopardy, sitting in that position—several steamers had passed us in the day—there was a little swell with them,
but I did not consider there was any danger—the chairs were standing in the bottom of the punt—the part on which we sat was above the water—I should think the side of the punt was not more than four or fire inches from the water—I have been fishing there at all times of the tide in the same way—several other persons were fishing in the river—there had been a boat about thirty yards from us—I do not know whether it was there at the time—I saw one afterwards about 150 yards off, and my impression is, there was one between that and us, but I cannot say—they were all in the same line in the river, down the stream, exactly in the same way—I cannot tell the width of the river there at high water—I cannot say whether Mr. Lewis was standing or sitting at the time we were struck by the steamer—the last time I saw him before he was in the water, he was standing—I am sure he did not fall in the water before the steamer struck the punt—I did not see him till he was in the water—I did not see how he went into the water—none of us were in the water beside—a little boy, I believe, was thrown into the water—he took hold of the punt—I do not know how he saved himself—he was wet through—I cannot say whether the stream is deeper on the Surrey side than the Middlesex, but I presume, from all the boats passing us on the Surrey side, that it is deeper there—I cannot say whether or not the steamer had any motion of the wheel when she touched the punt—I have always seen captains of steam-boats waving their hand to the man at the wheel—but I do not understand which way it means to direct him—I never went to Richmond by a steamer.
JURY. Q. I think you said you went on shore from the morning till the afternoon? A. Yes, we lunched at Mr. Lewis's house—we drank a glass of wine at lunch, but nothing more.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you mean you only drank one? A. I cannot say—I only drank one as far as I remember—I had drank no more than was rational to drink on such occasions—I had been taking a glass of wine and some ale—no more than my proper quantity—it was in the skiff I rowed about to see if I could find the body.
MR. DOANE. Q. You have said you were athwart the stream, was that the usual way? A. It was the way I have always been—I have fished twice this year, and have been accustomed to go down there every year—I have seen other punts moored in that way—I was not in the least affected by what I took at Mr. Lewis's, nor were any of my friends.
JURY. Q. What did Undey say or do, which made you suspect him to be in liquor? A. His manner was as if he had been drinking, and his breath smelt very much of liquor—he did not make any low remark, but it was his demeanor altogether which induced me to think so.
SAMUEL WEST . I am an artist, and live in Berners-street. I was one of the fishing party in the pun—I have not been in the habit of fishing there—I had been ashore in the course of the day, and went on board again about four o'clock—we had been to the house of Mr. Lewis's father, at Richmond, and taken some refreshment—none of us were in the least affected by what we drank—about half-past five, while sitting in the punt, I heard a noise of a steamer approaching—I looked round, and saw a steamer coming from behind—she was about thirty yards from us when I first saw her—she did not appear to be bearing on the punt at first—the bow of the vessel was going in a direction towards Richmond-green, about parallel with the Surrey shore—I think if she had kept that course, she
would have cleared the punt, but the Ride of the paddle-box struck the punt—she appeared to come more towards us before she struck the punt—that appeared to be from altering her course—I did not perceive any thing between her and the Surrey shore which made it necessary for her to alter her course—I had observed two or three steamers going up in the morning— their passage between us and the shore made the punt rock a little, from the swell—I did not notice any thing done on board the steamer before the blow was struck—the punt was very much shaken—I was thrown down—the punt tilted, and shipped some water, and was separated from her moorings—when I got up I observed Mr. Lewis in the water—efforts were used to save him, but he was quite dead when he was got out of the river afterwards—the steamer was going at about the ordinary rate when she came up to us—she bad the tide with her—my impression is, that her I wheels were in motion, but I cannot be certain—she was stopped as soon after the collision took place as she reasonably could be—I did not notice whether the action of her wheels was reversed—she stopped, and remained there some ten minutes, then went back towards Richmond bridge—I afterwards saw another steamer lying at the landing called "the Pigeons," above Richmond bridge—that is about one hundred yards from where our punt was moored—I did not notice whether she was in motion or lying there, nor whether she was close to the shore.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I forgot to ask Mr. Cotton whether he was examined before the Coroner? A. Yes, and so was I.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Can you say whether the stem or stern of the vessel was projecting into the river? A. I did not notice—there were some other gentlemen fishing there towards the meadow, and there were some gentlemen between us, nearer the bridge, lower down the river—I think they were a little above the Pigeons public-house—I am not certain whether they were there at the time of the accident—our moorning was a couple of stakes driven into the bed of the river—there was another punt before us during the morning—between us and the bridge—there was none above us—if there Were two, they were both below us—I did not observe my friend go into the water—I cannot tell whether he was sitting in the chair, or standing up—I do not "know whether any of us were standing up at the time we were struck—I cannot for certain say whether I was, but I think I was sitting down—I do not think the punt would take in water by a person standing up from a chair, and stepping over the gunwale, as the bottom was so broad—I have not had the opportunity of examining the punt, and cannot tell whether it was injured—the punt was, struck by the paddle-box end—the end of the case, outside the wheel.
MR. BODKIN. Q. If she had kept a little more off, she would have escaped you? A. Eight inches I think would have saved us.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you observe the captain wave his hand?' A. No—before this I had observed the vessel change her course more toward us—I did not apprehend any danger—she changed her course more towards the Middlesex side—I think it was three-quarters of an hour before Mr. Lewis was taken out of the water.
THOMAS CHARLES GARLIN . I was residing at Rose-cottage hotel, Rich-mond, on Monday, the 16th of August. On that evening I came down to Twickenham by the Richmond—I do not know who was acting as captain I saw the prisoner Undey on board—I was sitting on a bench behind
the paddle-box, on the side on which the punt was—the Middlesex side—nothing occurred till we got to the Duke of Buccleugh's—we were aboot half way opposite the duke's grounds before I saw any thing—I heard something on board, such as "an accident," some words to that effect—I looked over the side of the steamer, and saw a punt, one end of it in, and the other side a little tilted, pushed down to the water, and the water running into it—at that time a young gentleman fell out back wards into the water—I cannot say whether he had been standing or sitting—I saw him almost on the horizontal, certainly out of the perpendicular—I had felt no concussion—a large steamer in going against a small punt would not mike a concussion to be felt—the steamer was backed quite as soon as could reasonably be done—I saw Undey after the accident standing by the side of Dakers at the wheel—Dakers seemed to hold the wheel.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. If the steamer had been coming stem on, with any power on her, must she not have cut the punt in two? A. She must have struck her in the middle to cut her—if she had come sufficiently on the punt, I should think she must have run over her—a gentleman by my side said there was an accident, and I immediately looked over the side—the swell was very trifling indeed at the time—when the power is taken off, the impetus will cause the vessel to continue to go on some time, especially if the tide is with her—I have not travelled much by these steamers for some years—I know nothing of either of the prison. ers—I do not believe Undey was intoxicated at the time—I observed him then and afterwards—I saw the Vivid lying off the Pigeons public-house—we were backing when I observed her, after the accident—her prow was then to the shore, and her stern towards the river—she seemed about the same size as the Richmond —I should lay the Richmond had between thirty and forty passengers—the passengers belonging to the Vivid seemed to have landed at the time I saw her.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you examined before the Coroner? A. Yes—after the accident I asked the captain to change me a half-sovereign—he referred me to the stewardess—she gave me change, but no sixpence, which I wanted to pay my fare, and was obliged to ask for sixpence—the captain, the mate, and somebody else were standing by—I asked for sixpence, not addressing myself to either, and the captain took two sixpences out of his pocket, gave me one, and took my shilling from which I infer he was nothing like intoxicated—I have always had the same opinion.
MR. DOANE. Q. Can you say whether he appeared to hare been drinking? A. Not to me—there appeared nothing singular about him—I cannot say whether the wheels of the steamer were in motion at the tine I saw the punt—they must have been reversed when she was backed, of course, but I do not know whether they were in motion at the time of the accident—at the time the Richmond began to be backed I should judge she was about 100 yards from the Vivid.
EDWARD DICKENSON . I am a surgeon, and live at Kew Bridge. I was one of the fishing party in the wherry—it was not a skiff—I noticed the Richmond before she struck the punt—to the best of my recollection I first saw her about forty yards from the punt—she was standing down, I thought, inside of us, I mean the punt and wherry, for we were all fastened down—I judged that her course would clear the punt—I noticed a man on the larboard paddle-box, which was nearest to us, and thought he was making signal—
be waved his band, apparently guiding the man who was steering—I have been on board steamers, and seen how they are navigated—at the time she struck the punt, I think the wheels were not in motion—they were so when first saw her—I saw her paddle-box strike the punt—it tilted the punt, and threw the deceased into the river—when I first saw the steamer, she was coming down the stream, and, as I thought, taking a course which would clear the punt—I did not observe any alteration in her course—my idea was that the man on the paddle-box was making signals to put into the Richmond shore, as if to avoid us, but I did not observe any alteration in the course—I cannot tell whether the course was altered or not—the bow of the vessel cleared us entirely, and the greater portion of the paddie-box—a steamer had gone down before, pretty nearly in the same course as this was coming, but I do not know what became of it—I cannot exactly recollect how long before that was, but one had passed—I did not notice where she or any other steamer was at the time the collision took place, but I afterwards saw there were two steamers; the one which caused the accident, and another—I understand the distance between the punt and wherry and the other steamer has since been measured, and it was about one hundred yards—when I saw that steamer she was in motion, and I I thiok she was coming up—I thought she was coming to give us assistance at the time, but I did not see enough to judge.
Q. Can you tell whether there was any thing in the motion or position of that steamer to make it necessary for this steamer to keep nearer to your punt than it would otherwise have done? A. did not take notice—I I did not go ashore to lunch at Mr. Lewis's—I remained in the wherry.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You were examined before the Coroner? A. Yes.
GEORGE HOWARD . I am a waterman, and have been so about eleven year. The deceased engaged a punt of me on the morning of the 16th of August, which I moored off the Duke of Buccleugh's premises, about forty yards from them—it was athwart the stream—the Surrey end of the punt was not quite forty yards from the Duke's wall—I was present when the distance was measured—I am sure the spot where the measurement took place, was where I moored the punt—I have been frequently in the habit of mooring punts for the last eleven years—I can tell the place where I moor them from signs on the bank—the current is on the Surrey side—where we fished that day is three feet two deep at low water—the channel of the river is between that spot and the Duke of Buccleugh's—the large boats, the west country barges, and steamers, pass between the Duke of Buccleugh's and the punt—I have observed that in my experience as a waterman—there was a wherry with the punt—it was not fastened to the punt, it was fastened to a chain attached to the stake the punt was moored to—I have moored punts at the same place for the last eleven years—I left the gentlemen at half-past nine o'clock in the morning—I was not there when the accident happened—I went on board just before four, and left the punt moored secure in the usual way, athwart the stream—about five was returning to the punt, and met it coming down the stream with her stern down, and the head cocked up, and water in the stern—I saw the Richmond steamer—steamers always take the course between the punt and the Duke of Buccleugh's, coming to the channel—I saw the steamer below the punt when the accident was over—the wheels were not in motion —she was swinging down—Dakers, the mate, was at the wheel—I
have been on board the Richmond several times—in the summer time perhaps once a week—a person at the wheel of the Richmond could see a punt about fifty yards off, in my judgment—I saw the Vivid alongside the Duke of Buccleugh's lawn, just above the Pigeons'landing-place—I saw the punt afterwards—no damage whatever was done to her—I afterwards saw the body of the deceased—it was refused to be taken in at the Pigeons, and also at the White Cross.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were you hired along with your boat? A. No, it is not usual to have a waterman in the punts when young gentlemen go fishing—it is occasionally—if the stake which held the end of the boat had been pulled up, the action of the boat might have been to I swing with her bow down the stream—I was examined before the Coroner.
JURY. Q. Was the punt in the same place at five o'clock, as where you placed it at nine? A. The punt was, but the stern-pole was left—the hind pole was about twenty yards from where it had been—the punt had been shifted in the course of the day, but not the poles, and the punt had been replaced in the position it was in before.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When you saw the Vivid, she had got rid of her passengers, and was going up by the Duke of Boccleugh's? A. Yes, just above the pier—the pole of the punt had been shifted in the course of the morning by the wherry coming alongside—it had broken the rope, and then she swung down the stream—if I had been on board, the steam boats pass so frequently, I should not have looked out for any accident, and I Bright have been doing something to the tackle—if I had seen the Richmond coming, and had lifted up either of the poles, the punt would have swung round, and cleared the steamer altogether—a man at the wheel of the steamer could see about fifty yards before the vessel's bow—if there was any thing between the stern of the vessel and the paddle-box, I do not believe he could see—I believe that is the reason why a person is on the paddle box to guide him how to steer.
MR. DOANE. Q. Was there any thing to prevent a person at the wheel seeing there was room enough on the Surrey side of the steamer? A. Not that I know of—the stern-pole to which the punt was fixed was removed at the flood tide, about half-past three o'clock—I put it to rights at four, before I left it.
CHARLES LANGSTON . I am a constable of Richmond. I know the place pointed out by Howard, where the punt was moored—it is in the parish of Richmond—I measured from the Duke of Buccleugh's grounds on the Surrey side of the river, to a pole pointed out by Howard, and the distance was forty-three yards and upwards—I have known that part of the river all my life—I have seen punts moored there repeatedly, and have fished there myself—they are generally moored across the stream, fastened with two poles—I measured the distance from that place to the Pigeon landing-place, and it was one hundred yards and upwards—there is a jetty at the Pigeons, which projects into the river about thirty-four feet—I messured it since I gave evidence before the Magistrate—a vessel lying there when it comes in to land passengers would lie outside that thirty four feet.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What was the length of the Vivid? A. I do not know, it was a very long boat—I think it was low water the following morning, when I measured.
MR. BODKIN. Q. The distance would be the same, whether high or low water? A. It would.
COURT. Q. Did you measure to the Duke of Buccleugh'a wall, or what part? A. To the head of the wall, the planking—I measured as far as the water would reach at high tide.
JAMES JENKINS . I am a police-sergeant. My attention was called on this evening to the accident on the river—I saw Mr. Cotton, and went with him, at his request, on board the Richmond—the prisoner Undey was pointed out as the captain—I asked him if he was captain of the vessel—he said he was—I said, Mr. Cotton, the gentleman with me, had given him in charge, for running foul of a pant he was in, and causing the death of Edward Lewis—he said he had not charge of the vessel at the time the accident occurred—I told him he must go with me.—he said I should take the mate, as the mate had charge of the vessel at the time—Dakers was there, but said nothing then—he said at the station, in Undey's presence, that the captain was on the paddle-box at the time the accident occurred, and he was at the wheel—the captain smelt very strong of liquor—he appeered to have been drinking—he was a little the worse for liquor.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was he not very much agitated? A. He appeared so—when a person has been drinking he shows it in his countenance—agitation does not show itself in the countenance so much as drink—it may be visible.
KINGSTON MARKS . I am a police-sergeant. I received charge of the prisoners—Undey said he was in charge of the vessel, and Dakers that he was at the wheel.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You admitted them to bail, I believe? A. I did, about half an hour after they were given in charge—if there had been any appearance of intoxicatign on the part of the captain, I should not have done so—he was not intoxicated, to the best of my belief—there was no appearance of it, to my knowledge.
JURY to MR. GARLIN. Q. Did you observe whether there was a boy on board the steamer, who took the order from the captain to stop the engine? A. I did not observe any order given at all—all the passengers cried out "Stop her," or something—I heard a number of voices cry out "stop her"—the passengers were all very much horrified, and cried out "Stop her," "Ease her," and so on—as soon as the vessel could be stopped, it was done; as soon as the vessel occasioned the accident, or, probably just before—the persons on board the vessel, and those belonging to it, were in as great horror as the people in the punt.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder
[trialAccount t18410823-2300]
2300. JAMES FUSSELL was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecudion.
WILLIAM JOSEPH HAYES . I am a baker, and live in King-street. The prisoner was my journeyman—it was his duty to take out bread, and give an account every evening of what money he had received—if he received from Lucy Tate, for Mr. Powell, of Crosby-row, 7 1/2 d., he did not account for it; nor on the 26th of January, for 7 1/2 d.; nor on the 28th of January, for 7 1/2 d —I keep a book—on Tuesday, the 2nd of February, I stated to him my intention to send round to the customers, and next morning he went out, and I never saw him afterwards till he was apprehended—I have the book here—he accounted daily to me—on the 25th, 26th, and 28th of January, he gave an account of what he had taken out—here are
the entries—if a loaf is paid for, the pen is put across the amount; but he has made weekly customers of all these people—I put down what I receive each day in money—he does not put his initials to that—if I made a mistake, he would soon tell me—the bread the men take out is booked to them—here is the book, in which his cash is entered—on the 25th he paid me 12 s. 4 d., composed of 5 s. 9 d. 4 s. 9 d., &c.—there is not any amount of 7 1/2 d.—on the 26th he paid me 11 s. 2 d.—there is no 7 1/2 d. in that—that was the price for a 4lb. loaf on the 26th of January.
LUCY TATE . I am servant to Mr. John Powell, of Crosby-row. We dealt with Mr. Hayes, but do not now—I took in the bread—I always paid the prisoner regularly every day that he brought the bread—I do not remember any particular date, but it was always paid regularly daily—if we had a quartern loaf on the 25th of January I paid for it—he was in the habit of bringing bread from the 25th to the 28th of January, and I am quite certain I paid him on every occasion—I generally took in a quartern loaf.
MR. HATES re-examined. It appears by the account furnished by the prisoner to me, that Mr. Powell owed money, not as paying the money daily.
Prisoner. I used to pay him sometimes every other day, and at other times he let it run for a week. Witness. He paid me money perhaps every day—some days he would say there was nothing paid—I looked through my accounts, and he has not paid me this—he has made Mr. Powell a weekly customer—these entries are made in his presence—I could not prosecute him before, as I never saw him after he absconded.
GUILTY .— Confined Two Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18410823-2301]
2301. GEORGE WOOD and HENRY BLAGDON were indicted for stealing, on the 30th of June, 8 sacks, value 6 s.; 5 bushels of flour, value 2 l. 15 s.; 4 bushels of meal, called middling, value 20 s.; and 6 bushels of pollard, value 6 s.; the goods of Thomas Andrews: and JOHN SAVAGE , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to be stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS ANDREWS . I am a miller, and live at East Moulsey. I have a brother, George William Andrews, who is a corn and flour dealer, and lives at Brentford—I grind his corn for him. On the 24th and 25th of June I saw some sacks of pollard and flour counted out and delivered from my mill—I counted sixty sacks of pollard myself, and saw them put on board a lug-boat, called the Dreadnought, belonging to Mr. Page, a lighterman—Wood had the charge of the boat, and Blagdon was the boy attending it—it was partly laden on the 24th, and finished on the 25th—there should have been 120 sacks put on board for one customer, eighty for another, and fifty sacks of flour, which were to have been left at Messrs, Rayment's wharf, on our own account—I was not aware that any other sacks were taken from the mill than those I have mentioned, and they all reached their destination—I made out two of the delivery tickets for them myself—I employed Mr. Page, as a lighterman, and he employed Wood—I have seen a sack of flour, a sack of middlings, and a sack of pollard—one
of the sacks is mine, one my brother's, and the other is a sack that was on my premises—I can swear to my own sack and my brother's, and the pollard the middlings, and the flour which they contain, are such articles as I had in my premises, and I believe them to he mine—the middlings is in an unmanufactured state, it is too light for a sack of flour, and too heavy for a sack of pollard—it could not have been taken by mistake—the prisoners had not the opportunity of taking this property unless they had got into the mill, or the man in the mill had let them have it—the sack of floor is worth about 55 s., the pollard about 5 s. or 6 s., and the middling about 20 s.—my man Hurdle counted the remainder of the sacks after I left.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. How soon after the 25th of June did you see the three sacks? A. I think on the 30th—I have a large stock of sacks—as my brother manufactures goods at my mill I hare some hundreds of his sacks there—I have sacks at my customers in different parts, and I fear I lose a great many—I would swear to the contents of these three sacks if I am allowed to swear to samples—these middlings unmanufactured are so different to any thing that ever goes out of a mill, that I can swear to that more than any thing—there are various sorts of middlings, but this is not cleaned off, it ought not to have gone to any body in this state—my complaint is that there were more sacks on board this boat than there ought to have been—I have not known that on some occasions there has been two or three more sacks counted into a boat than there ought to have been—I have heard of bargemen saying there was not the quantity there ought to have been on board—we put in the number ordered, and if the bargeman says it is not right, we count it and make it right.
JAMES HURDLE . I am in Mr. Andrews's employ; Wood and Blagdon are in the employ of Page, the lighterman. On the 24th and 25th of June I counted sixty sacks of one sort of pollard, and eighty of another, and fifty sacks of flour—they were put into the Dreadnought for what I know—I did not stand by all the time—my master had counted the sacks before me.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. You did not see them all put In the boat? A. No—I saw them after they were in, but did not count them.
FRANCIS NASH . I am a wharfinger, at Pickle Herring wharf, Tooley-street, in the county of Surrey. On Wednesday, the 30th of June, I saw Page's lug-boat, the Dreadnought, alongside of my premises, at a little after, ten o'clock in the morning—Blagdon was on board of it alone—I desired him to move the lug-boat from alongside of my premises, and he said he was waiting for his mate—about an hour after that I saw Wood in company with Savage, who passed on the land side of my wharf, walking in the street together—I knew Savage before, and my suspicions were excited—I saw Wood get into the lug-boat—some porters were standing by, and some of them got into the lug-boat with him—Wood then pulled the tarpaulin off something that was in the boat—I saw him and one of the porters up-end a sack, which I supposed was a sack of flour, and they hove it on another porter's back—immediately after, before the porter was out of the boat, another sack, which I supposed to be flour, was up-ended, and placed on another porter's back—I then walked to the land side of my premises, and saw the "first sack come from off the slip way into the street,
on the porter's back; and shortly after that the second sack came—I then went and took the names on the sacks, and followed them to the end of Stoney-lane—Savage was following the two porters with the sacks, and they went to a van, which was standing opposite to Stanton's wharf, about 120 yards from the place of landing—I took the name of the van, and sent my son for a policeman, and desired him to come down Stoney-lane—a lad named Collins was with the van—a third sack was then brought and placed in the van, by the porter who brought the first sack—Savage assisted the porter in putting one of the sacks into the van, and he stood there superintending the loading of it—the sacks that were put into the van are now in a cart, outside this court—the name of "Collins, Vinegar-yard," was on the van—Savage is a professed dealer in marine stores—he lives by the water side, very near my premises—when the van had driyea off about fifty yards the policeman came—I then asked the boy Collins, who was driving it, where he was going with the flour—he said, "I don't know; that man (pointing to Savage, who was on the foot-path) has employed me"—I then turned round to Savage, and he said, "I know nothing at all about it; I have not employed him"—he was about to go away, when Collins desired the policeman to stop him—the policeman said be would take the van and Collins and Savage to the station, and he desired me to come—I said I would, but I would first go and secure the two men who landed the flour—I went, and the boat was gone—I called the police-bolt, and told the inspector—he told me to get into his boat—he rowed some distance, and got alongside the lug-boat—I pointed out Wood as the man who assisted to get out the flour—Wood said he had not been at pickle Herring at all, but he bad been at Bank-side delivering flour; but while the boat was rowing up, he said it was no use denying it, he had been at Pickle Herring, but Blagdon knew nothing about it.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You do not know whether Wood and Savage were talking? A. No—I saw them walking together on the land side of my premises, and I saw Savage stand before one of the porters, and push the sack into the van—any labouring man might have done that, who saw the porter wanted assistance—after Collins pointed out Savage as the man who had employed him, I said to Savage, "Where are you going with the flour?"—he said, "I do not know, I did not employ him"—Collins said, "Yes, you did"—I have known Savage seventeen or eighteen years.
HENRY COLLINS . I am the son of Samuel Collins; he lives in Vinegar-yard, and lets out vans. On the 30th of June, Savage came to my father's, about eleven in the morning—(I had known him before)—he wanted to know if I had got a horse and cart—I said, "No, but I had got the van "—he said that would do—I asked what it was for—he said to take two sacks of flour from Pickle Herring—I asked where they were to go to—he said by St. George's church—he said he wanted me directly, and I was to have 2 s. for the journey—he told me to go down as soon as I could, and he would go home and get his coat and come—I put the horse in the van, and went down to Pickle Herring—he was not there—I went on to the corner of the next entrance—he came to me, and came with me to Stoney-lane—he told me to draw up and turn round the van, he let the tail-board down, and said they would bring the flour to me —a porter then brought one sack, and then a second sack came, and Savage came with it—the man who brought the second sack turned round
with it to the van, and Savage put his band up to shove it in—Savage then told me to come on—he walked on, and I got out of the van to follow him—a man hallooed to me that he was coming with another sack—instead of stopping to take it, I ran round the corner to Mr. Savage to know if that was right—all that be said was, "Come on"—I went back to the man, and the third sack was put into the van—Savage had turned down Stoney-lane—he did not come back—I drove on, and when I got on by the brewer's gate, I said to Savage, "Will you get up?"—he said, "No"—I said, "Where am I to go to?"—he said, "Drive it home to your own place, and if any body asks you who hired you, do not tell them"—I told him I did not know what he meant, and just then, I saw the policeman coming—Savage was about to go away, and I told the policeman to take Savage, as I saw there was something wrong—Mr. Nash came up directly.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Is this the first little thing of this sort you have been in? A. Yes—I never bad' any thing to do with the police before—I was not going to take the van to our own house—I asked Savage again where I was to take it, because be had not told me any street or house to take it to, but only by St. George's church—I was examined at Union-ball, and I stated there that Savage told me to take it to our own house, and if any body asked me who hired me, not to tell them—I was seventeen years old last June—my father has one van and two carts—he was gone to Romford on that day—I was in custody on Wednesday when the policeman took me—I was in the station a few hours—I was bailed when I was before the Magistrate, and on the Monday, the Magistrate said I must come again on the Wednesday as a witness.
THOMAS THOMPSON ( police-constable M 78.) I was sent for on the 30th of June to go to Stoney-lane—I saw two sacks of flour in the van, and one of pollard—Collins was by the bone—Savage was on the foot-path—he was going away from the van when he saw me coming down—Collins told me to take him into custody—Mr. Nash was there at the time—I took the van and the sacks, and Collins and Savage.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had you received notice of this before? A. Yes, Mr. Nash sent his son to me—I asked Savage what they were—he said be did not know, he had nothing to do with them—it was from what Collins said that I took Savage.
JAMES FOGG . I am inspector of the Thames-police. On the 30th of June, I saw the Dreadnoughts lug-boat coming from Pickle Herring, and soon after I received notice from Nash, and went in pursuit of the lug-boat—it was then about a mile off, near to Rotherhithe—I found Wood and Blagdon in it—I asked them what they had been doing at Piekle Herring—they said they had not been at Pickle Herring at all—I asked where they had got the three sacks of flour that they landed there—they said they had landed no flour there—I took them into custody, and brought them up the river—on the way to the station, I said, "I know you have landed the flour there; where did it come from?"—they said they brought it from the mill—I said, "If you had it over, why did not you take it to the mill again?"—they said they were afraid of getting the people at the mill into trouble.
Cross-examined by MR. RYLAND. Q. Did they both speak with one voice? A. They both said three or four times that they bad landed no flour there—I then said they must go with me—I did not tell them
they had better tell all about it—I know it is customary with lightermen to take goods back if they hare too much—I have been thirty years on the river.
(Wood received a good character.)
WOOD — GUILTY. Aged 22.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Nine Months.
BLAGDON — GUILTY. Aged 74.— Recommended to mercy by the
Prosecutor and Jury.— Confined Two Months.
SAVAGE— GUILTY . Aged 74.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2305. JOHN BRISTOW was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of November, 3 planes, value 5 s.; 1 rule, value 1 s.; 1 square, value 1 s,; 1 bevel, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 gauge, value 3 s.; the goods of Robert Robinson, his master.
ROBERT ROBINSON . I am a carpenter, and live in Magdalen-street, Southwark. The prisoner was my journeyman—he had no tools of his own, and I allowed him to use my tools on my premises, but not to take them away—I missed the planes and other things stated—some of them are here—the rest I cannot find.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you been in the habit of pawning things? A. I have pawned many things on my own account, but I never suffered a man to do it—I never authorized the prisoner to pawn any thing, that I swear—I paid him 28 s. a week, and found him in tools, which is not usually done—I do not owe him any thing; on the contrary, he owes me money, and here is proof positive in his own handwriting—I never said, after he bad pawned tools for me, "Don't take any more tools; if you want money, ask me for it, and you shall have it;" nor any thing of the kind.
EDWARD MATTHEWS ( police-constable M 68.) I took the prisoner 09. the 14th of August—I told him it was for stealing Mr. Robinson's tools—he said he had them, and had made away with them—he gave me the duplicates, and I found these tools at the pawnbroker's—when the prisoner was in the station he fell on his knees, and begged forgiveness.
NOT GUILTY .

2309. WILLIAM CASEY was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of February, 1 yard of satin, value 12 s.; and 1 waistcoat, value 3 s.; the goods of James Gilson Foster, his master.
JAMES GILSON FOSTER . I am a tailor, and live at Aldgate. The prisoner was in my service—he left me on the 21st of February—I missed this satin and the waistcoat at different times—I can speak to the identity of them.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You missed one while he was with you? A. Yes, the satin, and after he was gone I missed the waist-coat—that was there after the satin was taken—I do not remember, while the prisoner was with me, my brother John requiring a saddle-horse to go to Stratford—this waistcoat is my make.
JOHN FOSTER . I am the prosecutor's brother. This is hit waistcoat—he never gave it to me.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever been in your brother's employ? A. I was there at the time this waistcoat was there—I was a tailor—the prisoner was groom—my brother was in the habit of wearing this waist-coat, perhaps for nine or ten months—I remember, on one occasion, requiring a horse to go to Romford—I asked the prisoner to get me the horse—he did get it—I did not give him this waistcoat for getting me the horse.
AMELIA BUTLER . The prisoner kept company with me for some time—we were likely to be married, and he has refused to marry me—I have seen him wear this waistcoat.
Cross-examined. Q. Where are you living now? A. With my father and mother—I knew a man named Bates some time ago—I left my father to be married to the prisoner—I think it was the week before Easter—I left in the evening—I know persons are not married after twelve o'clock—I never lived with Bates, nor with any body bat the prisoner—I staid with him three weeks—I told my father that the prisoner would not marry me—I told Mr. Foster that I had got a bit of this satin in my bonnet.
CHARLES STEWART . I am a police-constable. I took the prisoner at Mr. Lock's, at Lambeth, his last master's—I found this waistcoat there on the counter.
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Confined Nine Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

2311. MARTHA WILLIAMSON was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of July, 1 picture, value 6 s.; and 1 frame, value 10 s.; the goods of William Morley; and WILLIAM JOHNS for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.—2nd COUNT, for receiving of a certain evil-disposed person.
WILLIAM MORLEY . I am a picture-frame maker, and live in Mountrow, Westminster-road. This picture and frame are mine—I made the frame, and missed it about seven weeks ago out of my shop—I am certain I never sold it.
Cross-examined MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you not state that it was lost from outside your shop? A. I cannot say exactly whether it was outside or in.
WILLIAM HUMPHREYS ( police-constable M 127.) On the 2nd of August I saw the two prisoners fighting in Earl's-court—Williamson called me, and I separated them—she begged I would take the man into custody for assaulting her—they lived together—he went up stairs at their house, and I heard him breaking the things—I went up, and Williamson followed me—I found him breaking the things—he bad got this picture in his hand, and I took it from him—he said to Williamson, "Now, you b—, I will do you for this"—I took him to the station, and there Williamson said, in his presence, "I did not steal the picture, he stole it, and gave it me to carry"—he made no reply.
Cross-examined. Q. When you saw them first was not Johns violently beating the woman? A. Yes—he had got hold of her by the hair of her head.
Johns's Defence. I trust you will take my case into consideration—I have four helpless children totally dependent on me—I am innocent of any guilty knowledge of the picture—I told the woman I would not have any thing to do with it—I then went out of the house, and did not see the picture at all till I saw it in the officer's hand—I never knew any thing of the transaction.
WILLIAMSON— NOT GUILTY .
JOHNS— GUILTY Aged 42.

2312. MARTHA WILLIAMSON was again indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 5 gowns, value 21 s.; 5 printed books, value 3 s.; 1 saw, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2 shoes, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 chisel, value 1 s. 3 d.; and I brush, value 9 d.; the goods of Henry Morton, and another: and WILLIAM JOHNS , for receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.—2nd COUNT, for receiving of an evil disposed person.
HENRY MORTON . I am a pawnbroker, in the London-road; I have one partner; I know the prisoners, by seeing them outside the shop; they could have taken my property if they had a mind. On the 3rd of August the officer brought seventy-one duplicates—he asked if they were of property which had been stolen from us—some of them had reference to the articles stated in this indictment—the prisoner Johns had been at my house that morning, and I had engaged him to come the next day to line the shutten and doors with iron, but that afternoon the officer brought him and the duplicates—we then went to different shops, and found this property—afterwards, in company with the officer, searched the house where the prisoners lived, and found a great many articles of mine.
Johns. Q. Did you ever see me at your shop? A. I have seen you repeatedly pass, and I saw you that morning, when you came for the work—I cannot say whether I have seen Williamson in the shop.
CHARLES RUSSELL . I am in the employ of Mr. Stevens, a pawnbroker, I have a dress which I took in of Williamson—I advanced 2 s. on it.
WILLIAM HUMPHREYS . I asked Johns, at the station, if he had any duplicates—he said he put them into the box—we went back to his lodgings and found them.
Johns. Q. Where did you find them? A. One in the salt-jar and two in the warming-pan—one in a new frying-pan, and the others in a box.
Johns. I know nothing of any thing that was taken, and never did take anything.
WILLIAMSON— NOT GUILTY .
JOHNS— GUILTY . Aged 42, on the 2nd COUNT.— Transported for Seven Years.

2314. ELIZABETH HILL was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of March, 1 scarf, value 30 s., and 1 napkin, value 6 d.; the goods of William Webb.
FRANCES WEBB . I am the wife of William Webb—we live in Great Plant-street, Dover-road—the prisoner and her husband lodged with me, and five days after they left I missed a duplicate of a scarf and napkin—I went to the pawnbroker, and found they had been taken out—these produced are the articles—I know the scarf by these stains on the border.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What were they made by? A. I cannot tell—I believe the prisoner stained it—it was not stained previous to my lending it to her—I lent it her in the winter, to go and see her sister-in-law, in Leather-lane—I pawned it on the 16th of February, and put the duplicate into a pocket-book, in the end drawer in my bed-room—I bought this napkin, and made it myself—there is no mark on it, but I have one that corresponds with it—she lodged three months with me.
SOLOMON GARBATT ( police constable M 70.) On the 15th of July I went with Mr. Webb to a house in Leather lane—the prisoner was there—she was pointed out to me, and I took her—Mr. Webb went up stain, and brought down this scarf, which he said he could identify.
WILLIAM WEBB . I am the owner of this property. I went to Mr. Savant's, in Leather-lane—I went up stairs with him—he said, "If there is any thing here belonging to you, of course you can identify it"—I looked round, and saw this scarf the prisoner was down stairs—I came
down, and said to her, "Here is my scarf"—she denied it, and said she bought it for 7 s. 6 d.
Cross-examined. Q. Did it appear that she had it in wear? A. saw it hang on a chair back—I know it by a stain on the border.
THOMAS DICKER . I am a pawnbroker. I recollect taking in a scarf shawl wrapped in a napkin from Mrs. Webb on the 16th of February—it was taken out again on the 13th of March.

2316. DAVID DAVIS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of July 1 shawl, value 1 l. 5 s.; 1 stock, value 5 s.; 3 pairs of gloves, value 7 s.; and 7 handkerchiefs, value 1 l. 8 s.; the goods of Frederick Charles Burnham Blackwell, his master.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK CHARLES BURNHAM BLACKWELL . I live at Camberwell The prisoner had been in my employ—he came on the 19th of July—Lewis and Webster had been in my employ fora short time—Webster came in my employ on the 10th of July—then Lewis on the 12th—Webster left on the 19th—I questioned Lewis, and in consequence of something that occurred I had Davis taken up at Croydon—he was taken to the Rising Sun public-home—I I went there—I told him from what Lewis had stated to me he was guilty I of robbing me—he immediately turned very pale, and wished to leave the room with me—the policeman declined allowing that—the prisoner then walked to the other end of the room, and requested I would follow him—I did so—he said, "It is done, I can't help it; Lewis and Webster are the causes of it; I have only robbed you to the amount of 3 l., bat Lewis and Webster have robbed you to a considerable amount"—I had made him no promise before he said that—I told him 1 was in possession of the facts, and begged him to be as candid as he could, and tell me every thing, for Lewis had related the whole—he said he could make no particular confession till the Monday.
Cross-examined by MR. CLIRKSON. Q. What did you say about the Rising Sun public-house? A. I told him I wished to see him it the Rising Sun relative to some business I had with him—I did not invite him to take a glass at the Rising Sun—I said nothing about a glass when we got there, or before we got there—his wife was not there—my wife was there—we walked there together—I went into the parlour—there was a policeman of the letter P—I said to him, "Halloo, policeman, have you come from London?"—I had left London, and directed him to come down—I had called previously, and he had not come—I ordered a pint of half-and-half—the prisoner did not have any of that, to my knowledge —it was ordered for him if he liked to have it—we did not all get into one box on the railway—I did not say, "If you speak every thing you know, you shall get clear"—I did not say, "Have you robbed me of 10 l.?"—he did not say not 10 l., nor 3 l., nor any thing—I accompanied the officer when the prisoner's premises were searched—I do not know how
I many things were taken that do not belong to me—the officer took them supposing them to be mine, but I do not claim them.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. There was a good deal of property you did claim?
A. Yes.
JOHN DUDLEY ( police-constable P 263.) I went to the prisoner's house and searched it—he gave his address at the station to the inspector, "No. 2, Agnes-street, Waterloo-road"—I was present—I found three pairs of gloves, a stock, shawl, and three duplicates, which concern this indictment—nothing else—I was at the Rising Sun—I waited there till the prisoner came in company with the prosecutor—he asked the prosecutor to go out—I prevented it—I saw him go to the other end of the room—they were not near enough for me to hear what they said—in walking to the station the prosecutor accused him of robbing him—the prisoner said, "You have been robbed of as much as 1 l. a day besides goods."
THOMAS LEWIS ( a prisoner.) I was in the employ of the prosecutor—we robbed him—the first parcel we sent to Davis's house was on the 30th of July—I had been in the prosecutor's house a month Wore this—these drab shawls, kid gloves, stocks, and handkerchiefs, Davis packed up, and sent to his house—I was engaged at the time, or I should have helped him—they were sent by the Parcels Delivery Company—we did not let the prosecutor know we were going to rob him.
Cross-examined. Q. Show us the marks of them? A. This is the milk on the stock E N, which means 2 s. 6 d.—there is no mark on the others.
JOHN HENRY ALDRIDGE . I am a pawnbroker, in Hoi ling-street. I produce seven handkerchiefs pawned with me for 16 s., on the 24th of July, think by the prisoner—I am not certain—I gate this duplicate for them.
JOHN DUDLEY re-examined. I found this duplicate in the prisoner's house.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Seven Tears.
[trialAccount t18410823-2317]
2317. JOSEPH HENRY WEBSTER was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of July, 1 shawl, value 1 l. 2 s.; and 8 yards of lawn, value 12 s.; the goods of Frederick Charles Burnham Blackwell, his master.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES CRIPPS . I am a pawnbroker. I produce a shawl and eight yards of lawn, which were pledged at my shop, on the 19th of July, in the name of John Williams, by the prisoner—I am rare he is the man—I gave him two duplicates.
THOMAS SAUNDERS ( police-constable P 184.) I took the prisoner on the 7th of August—Mrs. Blackwell told him she gave him in custody for robbing them—he did not say any thing—I searched the prisoner's house, and found many stockings and collars—I found no duplicates.
FREDERICK CHARLES BURNHAM BLACKWELL . I believe this shawl to be mine—I have similar shawls in my house, and have missed one—this lawn bears my private mark—I can swear to this—I did not authorise the prisoner to pawn any of my new things.
THOMAS LEWIS ( a prisoner.) I remember the prisoner coming to my master's house after he had left his service—he slept that night in the water-closet—he had three shawls given him by Davis—I should not
know them again—he took them away—I know nothing of any lawn being taken away by the prisoner.
Prisoner to MRS. BLACKWELL. Q. Did I not ask you what I was given in custody for? A. Yes; I told you for robbery, connected with the other young men—you denied any knowledge of it—you stated, on your way to the station, that you had received intelligence from Mr. Wilson that Mr. Blackwell had been robbed.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you know of his coming to sleep in the house? A. We heard it next morning.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner complain that your husband had been robbed? A. No—my husband's sister said he was there, and I sent for a policeman.
Prisoner's Defence. I had nothing at all to do with the robbery, Mr. Wilson first told me of it. I said I was very sorry to hear it, as Mr. Blackwell had been unwell; I would go down and see him.
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Transported for Seven Years.

2319. JOSEPH RICHARDSON was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of August, 2 shirts, value 4 s.; 1 jacket, value 1 l.; 1 waistcoat, value 3 s.; the goods of Robert Jones; 2 shirts, value 4 s.; 1 hammock, value 5 s.; and 2 clews, value 1 s.; the goods of Henry Budd, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
ROBERT JONES . I am a seaman, on board the schooner Fame, which is lying in the Thames. The prisoner was a seaman on board—he left it about three o'clock yesterday morning, without notice—we then all searched our chests—I missed a jacket, waistcoat, and two shirts.
HENRY BUDD . I was on board the vessel—I missed a hammock, trowsers, and two clews, which had been at the head of my bed—the prisoner had left the things that he has on now on board, and he came for them yesterday evening—I had not missed my things at that time.
JAMBS FOGG . I am a Thames Police-inspector. Jones brought the prisoner to our office, and charged him with having stolen his jacket, waist-coat, and two shirts—the prisoner said he had not taken them, and had not seen them, nor knew nothing about them—one of our men said to him, "I saw you before the Lord Mayor yesterday morning, with these things on"—I said, "Then lock him up"—he said, "The jacket is all safe"—I said, "Where is the waistcoat?" and he gave me the duplicate of it from inside his stocking.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going along the highway, and fell in with two shipmates. I did not feel myself satisfied with the schooner I was in, and they told me of a vessel going to Shields—I took the jacket and waistcoat in the room of my own.
GUILTY .* Aged 26.— Transported for Ten Years.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, SEPTEMBER 20 TH, 1841.
